### 119_doc_engine_event_eventQueue_h

<a id="119-doc-engine-event-eventqueue-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/119_doc_engine_event_eventQueue_h/docu.output.md`



## EventQueue

**Role:** `EventQueue` is a thread-safe FIFO buffer that decouples background producer threads (AI request handlers, webhook callbacks, file watchers, Python callbacks, etc.) from the main thread's event-dispatch loop. Background threads push `Event` objects into the queue under a mutex; the main thread drains those events periodically and dispatches them without ever needing to share direct state with the producers.

**Public API:**

- `Push(EventPtr event) → void` — Moves a `shared_ptr<Event>` onto the back of the internal `std::queue`. Acquires `m_QueueAccessMutex` for the duration of the push. Callers on any thread may call this concurrently. The moved-from `EventPtr` argument is empty after the call.

- `Pop() → EventPtr` — Removes and returns the front event, or returns `nullptr` if the queue is empty. Acquires the mutex for the entire operation. Intended for single-event retrieval; prefer `PopAll` for batch draining on the main thread.

- `PopAll() → std::vector<EventPtr>` — Drains the entire queue in one mutex acquisition, returning all pending events as a vector. This is the preferred call site for the main-thread tick loop because it minimises lock contention: the mutex is held only while swapping out the internal queue contents, and the returned vector is processed after the lock is released.

- `Size() → size_t` — Returns the number of events currently in the queue under the mutex. The value is stale the moment it is returned; treat it as a snapshot only.

**Collaborators:**

- `AIAssistant::Event` (`event/event.h`) — the polymorphic base type for every event in the system. `EventQueue` stores and transfers ownership of `Event` instances exclusively through `std::shared_ptr<Event>` (`EventPtr`). The queue itself has no knowledge of event subtypes.
- Main-thread dispatch loop (caller) — expected to call `PopAll()` on a regular tick or poll interval to forward events to their handlers.
- Any background subsystem that produces events (AI response callbacks, webhook receivers, file-watcher threads, embedded Python callbacks) — these call `Push()` from their respective threads.

**Threading / lifetime:**

All four public methods are safe to call from any thread simultaneously; every access to `m_Queue` is serialised through `m_QueueAccessMutex`. The canonical usage pattern is many-writers / one-reader: multiple background threads push, and a single main thread drains via `PopAll()`. Nothing in the implementation enforces single-reader discipline, but calling `Pop()` or `PopAll()` from multiple threads simultaneously is safe mechanically — callers just need to be aware events will be split arbitrarily between them. `EventQueue` objects are plain value types with no special construction or destruction requirements; they may be heap- or stack-allocated and should outlive all threads that hold a pointer to them. Ownership of the `EventPtr` values transfers out of the queue on `Pop`/`PopAll`; if no caller retains the returned `shared_ptr`, the `Event` is destroyed immediately.

**Notes:**

- `PopAll()` is strictly preferable to repeated `Pop()` calls in a drain loop because it holds the mutex for one contiguous sweep rather than re-acquiring it per event, reducing contention with producers.
- `Size()` acquires the mutex, so it is not free; avoid calling it in tight loops purely for logging.
- There is no capacity limit and no blocking wait. If producers outpace consumers the queue grows without bound; the main-thread tick rate must be sufficient to keep up with expected event volume.
- `Push` takes its argument by value and `std::move`s it into the queue, so passing a named `shared_ptr` will leave the caller's handle null — pass by value intentionally or pass `std::move(ptr)` explicitly at the call site.

### 11_doc_application_cloud_cloudCircuitBreaker_h

<a id="11-doc-application-cloud-cloudcircuitbreaker-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/11_doc_application_cloud_cloudCircuitBreaker_h/docu.output.md`



## CloudCircuitBreaker

**Role:** `CloudCircuitBreaker` implements a per-named-connection circuit breaker to prevent failure cascading during cloud provider outages in the j9t AI workflow engine. It maintains an independent state machine for each logical connection (identified by an arbitrary string name) cycling through three states: Closed (normal pass-through), Open (all requests immediately short-circuited for a configurable cooldown period), and HalfOpen (a limited number of probe requests are allowed through to test recovery). The class is the authoritative source of truth for whether a cloud call should even be attempted, and it feeds the dashboard Cloud LED via the `m_EverSucceeded` flag that distinguishes a configured-but-never-proved connection from a genuinely healthy one.

---

**Public API:**

- `CloudCircuitBreaker()` — default-constructs with `Config` defaults: 5 failure threshold, 60-second cooldown, 1 HalfOpen probe.

- `CloudCircuitBreaker(Config config)` — constructs with explicit thresholds. `Config::m_FailureThreshold` controls how many consecutive failures trigger Open; `m_CooldownSeconds` is the minimum time spent in Open before transitioning to HalfOpen; `m_HalfOpenProbeCount` limits how many requests can pass as probes before a HalfOpen decision is forced.

- `AllowRequest(connectionName) → bool` — primary gate call. Returns `true` if the request may proceed, `false` if it must be short-circuited. Has a state-transition side effect: if the circuit is Open and the cooldown has elapsed, this call itself advances the state to HalfOpen and returns `true` for the first probe. In HalfOpen, it increments the internal probe counter and returns `false` once `m_HalfOpenProbeCount` probes are already in flight awaiting outcomes. Inserts a default-constructed `CircuitState` (Closed) for any previously unseen connection name.

- `RecordSuccess(connectionName)` — unconditionally resets the circuit to Closed, zeroes `m_ConsecutiveFailures` and `m_HalfOpenProbes`, and sets `m_EverSucceeded = true`. If the previous state was HalfOpen, emits a LOG_APP_INFO transition message. Must be called after every successful cloud operation on a connection.

- `RecordFailure(connectionName)` — increments `m_ConsecutiveFailures`. In Closed state, opens the circuit if the count reaches `m_FailureThreshold`, recording `m_OpenedAt` via `std::chrono::steady_clock::now()` and emitting a LOG_APP_WARN. In HalfOpen state, immediately transitions back to Open (probe failed) and resets `m_OpenedAt`. In Open state, increments the counter but takes no further action and does not reset `m_OpenedAt`, meaning the cooldown clock continues from when the circuit was first opened.

- `GetState(connectionName) → State` — returns the current `State` enum for the named connection. Returns `State::Closed` for any connection that has never been seen, without inserting it into the map.

- `StateToString(state) → std::string` — static helper mapping `State` enum values to `"closed"`, `"open"`, `"half_open"`, or `"unknown"`.

- `GetHealthSummary() → std::vector<ConnectionHealth>` — returns a snapshot of all tracked connections. Each `ConnectionHealth` entry carries the connection name, current state, consecutive failure count, and `m_EverSucceeded`. Iteration order is unspecified (backed by `std::unordered_map`).

- `HasEverSucceeded(connectionName) → bool` — returns `true` only if `RecordSuccess` has been called at least once for the named connection. Returns `false` for unknown connections without inserting them. Used by the dashboard to distinguish "unknown" from "healthy."

---

**Collaborators:**

- `engine.h` — pulled in by the `.cpp` for the `LOG_APP_INFO` / `LOG_APP_WARN` macros used for all state-transition logging. No other symbols from `engine.h` are used in this file.
- Dashboard / Cloud LED subsystem — consumes `GetHealthSummary()` and `HasEverSucceeded()` to determine whether to render a connection as unknown, healthy, or degraded. The `m_EverSucceeded` flag was introduced specifically for this consumer.
- Cloud task dispatch layer (JCWF cloud tasks, Test button handler) — must call `AllowRequest` before each outbound cloud call and then call exactly one of `RecordSuccess` or `RecordFailure` based on the outcome.

---

**Threading / lifetime:**

`CloudCircuitBreaker` is internally synchronized. Every public method (including the `const` query methods `GetState`, `GetHealthSummary`, `HasEverSucceeded`) acquires `m_Mutex` via `std::lock_guard`. Callers need no external locking and may call any method from any thread concurrently. The object does not manage any background threads or timers; the Open-to-HalfOpen timeout is evaluated lazily inside `AllowRequest` by comparing `std::chrono::steady_clock::now()` against the stored `m_OpenedAt`. Lifetime is not managed by this class; the owning component (the cloud dispatch layer) is responsible for ensuring the `CloudCircuitBreaker` instance outlives all threads that call into it.

---

**Notes:**

- Connection names are inserted on first use inside `AllowRequest` and `RecordFailure` (both use `operator[]`), but `GetState` and `HasEverSucceeded` use `find` and will not create phantom entries for speculative lookups.
- In HalfOpen state `AllowRequest` permits exactly `m_HalfOpenProbeCount` requests (tracked by `m_HalfOpenProbes`). Subsequent calls return `false` until a `RecordSuccess` or `RecordFailure` resolves the probe. With the default `m_HalfOpenProbeCount{1}`, only one concurrent probe is ever in flight.
- `RecordFailure` in the Open state does not reset `m_OpenedAt`. The cooldown always measures from when the circuit was first opened, not from the most recent failure, so repeated failures during Open do not extend the cooldown.
- `RecordSuccess` resets the circuit from any state unconditionally, including from Open (e.g., if a caller bypasses `AllowRequest`). Callers that want strict state-machine fidelity should always call `AllowRequest` first.
- The `m_EverSucceeded` flag is never cleared by `RecordFailure` or state transitions. Once set, it stays set for the lifetime of the object, even if the circuit subsequently opens repeatedly.
- `GetHealthSummary` returns a by-value snapshot under the lock; the snapshot is immediately stale after the lock is released, so callers must treat it as a point-in-time diagnostic, not a durable contract.

### 120_doc_engine_event_events_h

<a id="120-doc-engine-event-events-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/120_doc_engine_event_events_h/docu.output.md`



## event.h (Aggregate Event Header)

**Role:** This is a convenience umbrella header that aggregates all event-type headers used by the j9t workflow engine into a single include. It does not define any classes, functions, or type aliases of its own; its sole purpose is to allow consumers to pull in the complete set of engine event types — application, filesystem, engine, timer, and Python error events — with one `#include` directive rather than five separate ones.

**Public API:**

No symbols are declared in this file directly. All public API surface comes from the included headers:

- `applicationEvent.h` — declares the application-lifecycle event type(s).
- `filesystemEvent.h` — declares filesystem-change event type(s) used by the file-watching subsystem.
- `engineEvent.h` — declares core engine event type(s) for internal workflow signalling.
- `timerEvent.h` — declares timer-tick or scheduled-trigger event type(s).
- `pythonErrorEvent.h` — declares event type(s) carrying Python runtime error information from the embedded Python subsystem.

**Collaborators:** Any subsystem in j9t that needs to handle or dispatch events should include this header rather than individual event headers. Direct collaborators are the event dispatcher, the file watcher, the embedded Python bridge, and any timer-driven workflow step that emits or consumes these event types.

**Threading / lifetime:** No state is introduced here. Threading and lifetime rules are governed entirely by the individual event types declared in the included headers. Callers should consult those headers for ownership semantics (e.g., whether event objects are value types, heap-allocated, or reference-counted).

**Notes:** Because this header uses `#pragma once`, it is safe to include transitively from multiple translation units without duplication. Adding a new event category to the engine requires both creating its own header and adding a corresponding `#include` line here to keep the umbrella complete. Omitting that step will cause consumers of `event.h` to silently miss the new type.

### 121_doc_engine_event_filesystemEvent_h

<a id="121-doc-engine-event-filesystemevent-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/121_doc_engine_event_filesystemEvent_h/docu.output.md`



## FileSystemEvents (`event/filesystem_event.h`)

**Role:** Defines the three concrete file-system event types used by j9t's file-watching subsystem: `FileAddedEvent`, `FileRemovedEvent`, and `FileModifiedEvent`. All three inherit from the intermediate base `FileSystemEvent`, which itself inherits from `AIAssistant::Event`. These lightweight value types carry a single filesystem path and are dispatched through the engine's event bus whenever the file watcher detects a creation, deletion, or modification on a monitored path.

**Public API:**

- `FileSystemEvent(std::string path)` — Base constructor. Takes the affected filesystem path by value and stores it via `std::move`. All three concrete types inherit this constructor through `using FileSystemEvent::FileSystemEvent`.

- `GetPath() const → const std::string&` — Returns a const reference to the stored path. Valid for the lifetime of the event object. No side effects.

- `FileAddedEvent(std::string path)` — Constructs an event representing a newly created file or directory at `path`. Registers `EventType::FileAdded` via `EVENT_CLASS_TYPE` and `EventCategoryFileSys` via `EVENT_CLASS_CATEGORY`.

- `FileRemovedEvent(std::string path)` — Constructs an event representing a deleted file or directory at `path`. Registers `EventType::FileRemoved` and `EventCategoryFileSys`.

- `FileModifiedEvent(std::string path)` — Constructs an event representing a content or metadata change at `path`. Registers `EventType::FileModified` and `EventCategoryFileSys`.

**Collaborators:**

- `event/event.h` — Provides the `AIAssistant::Event` base class and the `EVENT_CLASS_TYPE` / `EVENT_CLASS_CATEGORY` macros. These macros expand to static and virtual methods (`GetStaticType`, `GetEventType`, `GetCategoryFlags`) that the event dispatcher uses for routing and filtering. Callers should review that header to understand the full interface contract.
- File-watching subsystem — Whatever component monitors the filesystem (not in this file) is the producer of these events. It constructs the appropriate event type and posts it to the event bus.
- Event dispatcher / event bus — The consumer side. Handlers registered for `EventCategoryFileSys` or a specific `EventType` (e.g., `FileModified`) will receive these events.

**Threading / lifetime:**

These are stack- or heap-allocated value objects with no internal synchronization. The path string is owned by the event object itself. If events are posted asynchronously to a queue or bus, callers must ensure the event object remains valid until it is consumed; this typically means heap-allocating via `std::make_shared<FileModifiedEvent>(path)` or equivalent, following whatever ownership convention the `AIAssistant::Event` base and bus impose. Check the event bus implementation for whether it copies, moves, or holds a pointer to the event.

**Notes:**

- `m_Path` is `protected`, not `private`, so subclasses can read it directly without going through `GetPath()`. This opens the field to further specialization but is not used by any of the three concrete types in this file.
- The `EVENT_CLASS_CATEGORY` macro encodes `EventCategoryFileSys` as a bitmask flag. Dispatcher logic filtering by category (rather than exact type) will match all three event types simultaneously, which is the expected behaviour for a generic file-system listener.
- No path normalization, validation, or existence checking is performed here. The path is whatever the file watcher supplies; callers must not assume it is absolute, canonical, or that the file still exists when the event is handled (particularly relevant for `FileRemovedEvent`).
- There is no payload beyond the path — no timestamps, inode numbers, old vs. new content, or move-destination fields. If richer metadata is needed, a new subclass or an extended event type would be required.

### 122_doc_engine_event_keyboardEvent_h

<a id="122-doc-engine-event-keyboardevent-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/122_doc_engine_event_keyboardEvent_h/docu.output.md`



## KeyPressedEvent

**Role:** `KeyPressedEvent` is a concrete event class within the `AIAssistant` namespace that represents a single keyboard key-press occurring in the application. It inherits from the base `Event` class and uses the engine's macro-driven event classification system (`EVENT_CLASS_TYPE` and `EVENT_CLASS_CATEGORY`) to register itself as a `KeyPressed` event belonging to the `EventCategoryKeyboard` category. Its sole responsibility is to carry the identity of the pressed key through the event dispatch pipeline.

**Public API:**

- `KeyPressedEvent(char key)` — explicit constructor; takes the ASCII character of the pressed key and stores it internally. No side effects beyond object initialisation. The `explicit` qualifier prevents accidental implicit conversion from `char`.
- `GetKey() const → char` — returns the character value of the key that was pressed. Read-only; no side effects.
- `ToString() const override → std::string` — produces a human-readable string of the form `"KeyPressedEvent: <key>"` suitable for logging or debugging. Overrides the virtual method declared in `Event`.
- `EVENT_CLASS_TYPE(KeyPressed)` — macro expansion; provides the static `GetStaticType()` and virtual `GetEventType()` methods that return the `EventType::KeyPressed` enum value, plus `GetName()` returning the stringified type name.
- `EVENT_CLASS_CATEGORY(EventCategoryKeyboard)` — macro expansion; provides `GetCategoryFlags()` returning the `EventCategoryKeyboard` bitmask, used by the dispatcher to filter events by category.

**Collaborators:**

- `event/event.h` — defines the `Event` base class, the `EventType` and `EventCategory` enums, and the `EVENT_CLASS_TYPE` / `EVENT_CLASS_CATEGORY` macros that this class depends on entirely. Readers must understand those macros to know the full set of methods this class exposes.
- The broader event dispatch system (typically an `EventDispatcher` class defined elsewhere) consumes `KeyPressedEvent` instances by matching on `EventType::KeyPressed` or the `EventCategoryKeyboard` flag.

**Threading / lifetime:**

`KeyPressedEvent` is a lightweight value-like object. It is typically constructed on the stack or the event queue at the point of input capture and passed by reference or pointer into the dispatch chain. The class itself contains no synchronisation primitives. Thread safety of construction, dispatch, and consumption is entirely the responsibility of the surrounding event queue or dispatcher. Ownership follows whatever policy the event system uses for `Event` base pointers; there is no internal heap allocation in this class.

**Notes:**

- The key is stored as a plain `char`, which limits the representable key space to the basic ASCII range. Extended keys (function keys, arrow keys, Unicode code points) cannot be represented by this class as written.
- Because both `EVENT_CLASS_TYPE` and `EVENT_CLASS_CATEGORY` are macros, the exact method signatures they emit are defined in `event/event.h`. Any change to those macros silently changes the API of this class.
- There is no key-repeat count or modifier-key state (shift, ctrl, alt) carried in this event. If the system needs that information, a separate event type or an extension to this class would be required.

### 123_doc_engine_event_pythonErrorEvent_h

<a id="123-doc-engine-event-pythonerrorevent-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/123_doc_engine_event_pythonErrorEvent_h/docu.output.md`



## PythonCrashedEvent

**Role:** `PythonCrashedEvent` is a lightweight event class that carries a diagnostic message when the embedded Python runtime experiences a fatal crash or unrecoverable error. It plugs into the j9t event system by inheriting from `Event` and declaring its type and category via the standard `EVENT_CLASS_TYPE` / `EVENT_CLASS_CATEGORY` macros, making it dispatchable through the engine's event bus like any other first-class event.

**Public API:**

- `PythonCrashedEvent(std::string message)` — Constructs the event, taking ownership of `message` via move. The string is expected to contain a human-readable description of the Python failure (e.g., an exception traceback, interpreter error string, or internal crash reason). No side effects beyond storing the message.
- `GetMessage() const → const std::string&` — Returns a reference to the stored crash message. The reference is valid for the lifetime of the event object.
- `EVENT_CLASS_TYPE(PythonCrashed)` — Macro-generated; provides `GetStaticType()`, `GetEventType()`, and `GetName()` returning the `EventType::PythonCrashed` discriminator used by the dispatcher to route this event to registered handlers.
- `EVENT_CLASS_CATEGORY(EventCategoryEngine)` — Macro-generated; assigns this event to the `EventCategoryEngine` bitmask category, controlling broad filtering before type-specific dispatch.

**Collaborators:**

- `event/event.h` — Provides the `Event` base class, `EVENT_CLASS_TYPE`, and `EVENT_CLASS_CATEGORY` macros that the entire event hierarchy depends on. The `EventType::PythonCrashed` enumerator must be declared there for this file to compile.
- **Embedded Python subsystem** — Whichever component wraps CPython or executes Python scripts is responsible for constructing and dispatching this event when the interpreter enters an unrecoverable state.
- **Event bus / dispatcher** — Receives the event and routes it to subscribed handlers, typically components that perform logging, user notification, or Python runtime restart logic.

**Threading / lifetime:**

`PythonCrashedEvent` is a value-semantic, stack- or heap-allocated object with no internal synchronisation. It is typically constructed on whatever thread detects the Python crash and then passed (by pointer or reference) into the event bus. Callers must ensure the event object outlives any synchronous dispatch call. If the event bus queues events across thread boundaries, the posting component must either heap-allocate the event and transfer ownership or guarantee the object remains valid until processing completes. No shared mutable state exists within the class itself.

**Notes:**

- The `EventCategoryEngine` category places this event alongside other internal-engine events rather than user-input or network categories; handlers that filter by category rather than exact type will pick it up alongside all other engine-level events.
- Because `GetMessage()` returns a `const std::string&`, callers that need to retain the message beyond the event's lifetime must copy it explicitly.
- There is no error code or structured data beyond the raw string; any structured information (Python exception type, traceback depth, etc.) must be serialised into the message by the producer before construction.
- The `EventType::PythonCrashed` enumerator must exist in the `EventType` enum defined in `event/event.h`; adding this class without updating that enum will cause a compile error from the `EVENT_CLASS_TYPE` macro expansion.

### 123_doc_engine_json_configChecker_h

<a id="123-doc-engine-json-configchecker-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/123_doc_engine_json_configChecker_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Log Injection / Path Disclosure
- **Location:** ConfigChecker::Check, approximately lines 68-93
- **Issue:** User-supplied values (such as queueFolderFilepath and workflowsFolder) are directly included in log messages and error outputs without sanitization.
- **Impact:** An attacker with control over config values could inject control characters or unexpected text into the logs or error output (e.g., escape sequences, log forging, misleading content), or reveal file system structure if these logs are exposed.
- **Fix:** Sanitize or escape user-controlled values (including file paths) before logging/output. Never allow user input to directly appear in structured logs.

### LOW Potential Information Leakage
- **Location:** ConfigChecker::Check, same section as above
- **Issue:** Full file paths and possibly internal configuration values are printed to stderr and log files.
- **Impact:** If error logs or console output are exposed to end-users or attackers (e.g., in a shared environment, log collection, or bug reports), they may disclose sensitive information about internal file system structure or configuration.
- **Fix:** Avoid exposing full path information or sensitive configuration in error messages or logs. Instead, provide generic error messages and log more detailed info only in secure, access-controlled locations.

---

No direct vulnerabilities in memory management, authentication, cryptography, injection (SQL/shell/HTTP), or unsafe deserialization are present in the code as shown. All corrections for missing/bad config values are reasonable and do not introduce security gaps in this snippet as provided.

If these logs are not externally accessible, risk is low; if logs could be observed by an attacker, tighten log content as above.

---

**No critical or high-severity security issues identified.**

### 124_doc_engine_event_timerEvent_h

<a id="124-doc-engine-event-timerevent-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/124_doc_engine_event_timerEvent_h/docu.output.md`



## TimerElapsedEvent

**Role:** `TimerElapsedEvent` is a lightweight, immutable event value type that represents the firing of a named timer within the j9t/AIAssistant event system. It inherits from `AIAssistant::Event` and carries a single integer timer identifier, allowing timer-driven subsystems (scheduled AI requests, polling loops, file-watch heartbeats, etc.) to dispatch typed events into the engine's event bus rather than invoking callbacks directly.

**Public API:**

- `TimerElapsedEvent(int timerID) → void` — Constructs the event, binding it to the timer identified by `timerID`. The integer is caller-defined and must be kept consistent between the timer registration site and any listeners that dispatch on it. No validation or range-checking is performed.
- `GetTickId() const → int` — Returns the `timerID` supplied at construction. This is the primary discriminator listeners use to determine which timer fired.
- `EVENT_CLASS_TYPE(TimerElapsed)` — Macro-generated static and virtual methods (typically `GetStaticType()`, `GetEventType()`, and `GetName()`) that return the `EventType::TimerElapsed` enumeration value and the string name `"TimerElapsed"`. Used by the event dispatcher for type-safe routing.
- `EVENT_CLASS_CATEGORY(EventCategoryTimer)` — Macro-generated `GetCategoryFlags() const` that returns the `EventCategoryTimer` bitmask, allowing category-level filtering without a full type match.

**Collaborators:**

- `AIAssistant::Event` (`event/event.h`) — The base class that defines the event interface, the `Handled` flag, and the contracts required by `EVENT_CLASS_TYPE` and `EVENT_CLASS_CATEGORY`. Any changes to the base event contract directly affect this class.
- Event dispatcher / event bus — Wherever in j9t the central dispatcher consumes `Event&` references, it will use `GetEventType()` and `GetCategoryFlags()` to route `TimerElapsedEvent` to registered timer listeners.
- Timer subsystem — The producer side; whatever component manages wall-clock or tick-based timers is responsible for constructing and posting `TimerElapsedEvent` instances with the correct `timerID`.

**Threading / lifetime:**

`TimerElapsedEvent` is a stack- or short-lived heap-allocated value with no internal synchronisation. It is typically constructed at the timer-fire site, passed by reference through the synchronous event dispatch chain, and then discarded. Callers must not retain a pointer or reference to an instance after the dispatch call returns. If the timer subsystem fires from a background thread, the caller is responsible for ensuring the event dispatch into the bus is thread-safe; this class itself provides no locking.

**Notes:**

- `timerID` has no engine-enforced uniqueness constraint. Collisions between separately registered timers sharing the same integer ID will cause unintended listeners to respond; IDs must be managed externally.
- The `Handled` flag inherited from `Event` follows standard j9t event semantics: a listener that sets it `true` signals that downstream listeners should skip processing, though enforcement depends on the dispatcher implementation.
- This file is a header-only definition (`#pragma once`, no corresponding `.cpp`). The entire class is inline and has no link-time footprint of its own.

### 124_doc_engine_json_configParser_h

<a id="124-doc-engine-json-configparser-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/124_doc_engine_json_configParser_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Information Disclosure of Sensitive TLS and Secret Fields in Logs
- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse` and `ConfigParser::ParseInterfaces` (various, e.g., line ~190, ~422, ~594)
- **Issue:** Sensitive values such as `TlsCert`, `TlsKey`, `keys_file`, `mcp_keys_file`, `TrustedProxyHeader`, and `TrustedRoleHeader` are logged in plaintext via LOG_CORE_INFO when parsed from the config.
- **Impact:** If logs are accessible to unauthorized parties, certificate material, key paths, or header-based trust secrets could be leaked, compromising TLS, authentication, and potentially giving attackers information for privilege escalation or MITM.
- **Fix:** Do not log the actual value of sensitive configuration items such as `TlsCert`, `TlsKey`, or any field that points to cryptographic secrets or trust indicators. Log only whether the field was set, its length, or that it was present, e.g., `LOG_CORE_INFO("TlsCert: [REDACTED] ({} bytes present)", engineConfig.m_TlsCert.size());`

---

### MEDIUM Unvalidated Path Input Enables Path Traversal
- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse`, assignment of `m_QueueFolderFilepath`, `m_WorkflowsFolderFilepath`, `m_KeysFilePath`, and `m_McpKeysFilePath` (lines ~166, ~172, ~230, ~361)
- **Issue:** Configuration file values are assigned directly to file path fields with no validation or restriction on the path (e.g., no check for relative paths, absolute paths, or access outside intended directories).
- **Impact:** A malicious config could set these to `../../etc/passwd` or similar, potentially leading to read/write outside the intended directory sandbox, especially if later code uses the fields unsafely.
- **Fix:** Implement path validation/sanitization before using/assigning any path in configuration. Ensure only allowed directories are accepted, and restrict usage of `..`, absolute paths, and symbolic links.

---

### MEDIUM No Enforcement of Numeric or Resource Limits in Configuration
- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse` (multiple, e.g., `max_threads`, `max_inflight_ai_calls`, `python_engines`, etc. ~178, ~325, ~353, ~370)
- **Issue:** Numeric values from config are assigned to resource-controlling fields (thread counts, inflight requests, Python engines, request body size, etc.) with little or no range checking (except `port` and `session_timeout_hours`).
- **Impact:** An attacker with control over the config can force the engine into allocating huge resources, causing uncontrolled memory, thread, or file creation, and leading to Denial of Service.
- **Fix:** Add reasonable upper and lower bounds to all such settings. For example, cap `max_threads` to a sane maximum (like 256), `maxInflightAiCalls`, `MaxRequestBodyMB`, etc. If exceeded, either fail parsing or clamp to defaults.

---

### LOW Use of Exception (catching all simdjson errors as warnings) May Obscure Malformed Input
- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse` (catch block near line ~412)
- **Issue:** For unknown fields in config, the code tries to stringify/log them, and on failure logs a warning, but parsing overall will succeed.
- **Impact:** Malformed or malicious input could be ignored, and unintended or invalid fields could silently pass, potentially opening avenue for confusion or deserialization vulnerabilities elsewhere.
- **Fix:** Consider treating unknown or unserializable fields as a parse failure, or introducing a stricter validation mode where unknown fields are flagged as errors.

---

### LOW No TLS Key/Certificate Validation Before Usage
- **Location:** `ConfigParser::Parse` lines ~287, ~294
- **Issue:** No check is performed to ensure values for `TlsCert` and `TlsKey` are not empty, are in valid PEM format, or refer to existing files.
- **Impact:** If left misconfigured, the server may start with invalid or missing TLS credentials, enabling downgrade or loss of encrypted channel.
- **Fix:** After assignment, check the field for reasonable content (e.g., file exists, contains valid certificate/private key), and fail config parse if invalid.

---

### LOW Potential JSON Injection in Logs
- **Location:** Logging of unescaped and unchecked config field values, e.g., `LOG_CORE_INFO("name: {}", name);` in `ParseInterfaces`.
- **Issue:** Arbitrary values from config files are logged without any sanitization or truncation.
- **Impact:** Malicious configuration could inject log lines, potentially confusing log parsers or, if logs are ingested elsewhere, enable log injection attacks.
- **Fix:** Sanitize input before logging by escaping control characters, limiting length, or using structured logging with explicit encoding.

---

### NONE
No memory safety, concurrency, cryptographic primitives, SSRF, injection, or insecure deserialization vulnerabilities were found within the present code. However, the issues above warrant attention.


---

### 125_doc_engine_input_keyboardInput_h

<a id="125-doc-engine-input-keyboardinput-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/125_doc_engine_input_keyboardInput_h/docu.output.md`



## KeyboardInput

**Role:** `KeyboardInput` is a thin, platform-abstracted input poller that runs a blocking keyboard-read loop on a dedicated thread-pool task. Its sole job is to translate raw terminal key presses into engine events (`KeyPressedEvent`, `EngineEvent::EngineEventShutdown`) and push them onto the central event queue via `Core::g_Core`. It is also the canonical trigger for an orderly engine shutdown when the user presses `q`/`Q`.

---

**Public API:**

- `Start() → void` — Arms the listener. Sets `m_Running = true` and submits `Listen()` as an async task to `Core::g_Core->GetThreadPool()`. The returned `std::future<void>` is stored in `m_ListenerTask`. If already running (`m_Running` is `true`) the call is a no-op; no second task is spawned.

- `Stop() → void` — Signals the listener loop to exit by setting `m_Running = false`, then blocks on `m_ListenerTask.wait()` until the background task returns. Logs `"Keyboard input stopped"` on clean exit. If not running, the call is a no-op.

---

**Collaborators:**

- `Core` / `Core::g_Core` (global singleton) — provides the thread pool used to run `Listen()`, the `RequestQuit()` shutdown signal, and `PushEvent()` to enqueue events.
- `EngineEvent` (`event/events.h`) — instantiated with `EngineEvent::EngineEventShutdown` when `q`/`Q` is pressed.
- `KeyPressedEvent` (`event/keyboardEvent.h`) — instantiated for every other printable character read; newlines (`\n`) and `EOF` are silently discarded on POSIX.
- `TerminalManager` (external, not included here) — on POSIX, `Listen()` relies on the terminal already being in cbreak/no-echo mode, set up by `TerminalManager` via ncurses. `KeyboardInput` itself does **not** manipulate `termios`.
- `Core::GetThreadPool().SubmitTask()` — the mechanism that runs `Listen()` asynchronously; the returned `std::future<void>` drives the `Stop()` join.

---

**Threading / lifetime:**

- `Listen()` runs on a thread-pool thread, not the calling thread. `Start()` and `Stop()` are intended to be called from the main/engine thread.
- `m_Running` is `std::atomic<bool>`, making the stop signal safe to write from one thread and read from the listener thread without a mutex.
- `Stop()` calls `m_ListenerTask.wait()`, which blocks the caller until the listener exits. On POSIX, the poll loop uses a 100 ms `select()` timeout, so the maximum drain time after `Stop()` is ~100 ms. On Windows, `_kbhit()` + `sleep_for(100ms)` gives the same bound.
- There is no protection against calling `Start()` concurrently from multiple threads; the `if (m_Running)` guard is not atomic with the subsequent assignments, so callers must serialize `Start()`/`Stop()` externally.
- `KeyboardInput` does not own the `Core` singleton; it must be alive (i.e., `Core::g_Core` must be valid) for the entire duration between `Start()` and `Stop()`.

---

**Notes:**

- **Headless / Docker safety:** On POSIX, `Listen()` calls `isatty(STDIN_FILENO)` before entering the loop. If stdin is not a TTY (e.g., a container without `-it`), the function logs and returns immediately, leaving `m_Running = true` but the future already resolved. A subsequent `Stop()` will still call `m_ListenerTask.wait()`, which returns immediately.
- **Terminal mode dependency:** The POSIX path assumes cbreak mode is active. If `TerminalManager` has not initialized ncurses, `getchar()` will block until `Enter` is pressed (cooked mode), making the 100 ms polling interval meaningless and potentially causing `Stop()` to hang until the user hits a key.
- **`\n` and `EOF` are silently dropped** on POSIX; they do not produce a `KeyPressedEvent`. On Windows, `_getch()` returns scan codes for special keys, which will be cast to `char` and wrapped in `KeyPressedEvent` without filtering — callers handling `KeyPressedEvent` should be prepared for non-printable values on Windows.
- **`q`/`Q` handling is destructive:** pressing either key calls `Core::g_Core->RequestQuit()` *and* pushes `EngineEventShutdown`, then breaks out of the loop. There is no way to intercept or cancel the shutdown from the event system after this point.
- The class has no copy or move semantics defined; the defaulted destructor does **not** call `Stop()`, so failing to call `Stop()` before destruction leaves `m_ListenerTask` in a joinable state, which will call `std::terminate` when the `std::future` destructor runs if the task is still live.


---

### 125_doc_engine_json_jsonHelper_h

<a id="125-doc-engine-json-jsonhelper-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/125_doc_engine_json_jsonHelper_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Potential Incomplete JSON String Escaping
- **Location:** JsonHelper::SanitizeForJson (approx. lines 19–60)
- **Issue:** The SanitizeForJson function appears to intend escaping text for safe JSON embedding, but it does not cover all control characters and does not perform UTF-8 or Unicode escaping for non-printable or special characters (\b, \f, and others), nor does it escape forward slashes or non-ASCII data. There is also a redundant `case ''` that serves no purpose.
- **Impact:** Malformed or unexpected JSON may be produced in edge cases, possibly enabling attackers to manipulate JSON structure (rare in practice but dangerous if assumptions are made downstream), or to inject control characters that could affect some parsers. Downstream logic expecting proper JSON may handle input unsafely or fail with unexpected structure, depending on usage.
- **Fix:** Use a well-tested JSON library for escaping (such as nlohmann::json or rapidjson). If hand-writing, add all required string escapes per RFC 8259, including \b, \f, and escaping control codes as `\u00XX` for chars < 0x20. Remove the `case ''` statement.

---

No critical or high-severity vulnerabilities found in this file.

### 126_doc_engine_json_configChecker_h

<a id="126-doc-engine-json-configchecker-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/126_doc_engine_json_configChecker_h/docu.output.md`



## ConfigChecker

**Role:** `ConfigChecker` is a validation and auto-correction pass that runs against a fully-parsed `ConfigParser::EngineConfig` before the engine starts. It enforces that mandatory filesystem paths exist, that the selected API interface is minimally sane (non-empty HTTPS URL, non-empty model name, non-`InvalidAPI` type), and — only when those hard requirements pass — silently clamps optional numeric fields (`m_MaxThreads`, `m_SleepDuration`, `m_MaxFileSizekB`, `m_MaxInflightAiCalls`, `m_PythonEngines`) to safe defaults. On failure it writes human-readable diagnostics to both the structured log and `stderr`, then marks `EngineConfig::m_ConfigValid` accordingly.

**Public API:**

- `Check(ConfigParser::EngineConfig& engineConfig) → bool` — The primary entry point. Takes a mutable reference to the already-parsed engine config and performs three hard checks in sequence: (1) `engineConfig.m_QueueFolderFilepath` is an existing directory, (2) `engineConfig.m_WorkflowsFolderFilepath` is an existing directory, (3) the `ApiInterface` at `engineConfig.m_ApiIndex` exists, has an HTTPS URL longer than `"https://"`, a non-empty model string, and an `InterfaceType` other than `InvalidAPI`. If all three pass, out-of-range optional fields are fixed in-place and `m_ConfigValid` is set to `true`. If any hard check fails, `m_ConfigValid` is set to `false`, per-failure error messages are emitted via `LOG_CORE_ERROR` and `fprintf(stderr, ...)`, and the method returns `false`. The struct is modified on success (clamped fields); on failure the struct is left largely unmodified except for `m_ConfigValid = false`. Calling `Check` resets `m_ConfigIsOk` to `true` at entry, so the object can be reused across multiple calls.

- `ConfigIsOk() const → bool` — Returns the cached result of the most recent `Check` call. Returns `false` if `Check` has never been called (default-initialised to `false`). Purely a read accessor; no side effects.

**Collaborators:**

- `ConfigParser::EngineConfig` (`json/configParser.h`) — The struct being validated and mutated. Relevant fields: `m_QueueFolderFilepath`, `m_WorkflowsFolderFilepath`, `m_ApiInterfaces`, `m_ApiIndex`, `m_InterfaceType`, `m_Url`, `m_Model`, `m_MaxThreads`, `m_SleepDuration`, `m_MaxFileSizekB`, `m_MaxInflightAiCalls`, `m_PythonEngines`, `m_ConfigValid`.
- `EngineCore::IsDirectory` (`auxiliary/file.h`) — Used to test whether the two path fields resolve to existing directories on disk. Any path that is missing or is a regular file will fail this check.
- `CORE_ASSERT` (`engine.h` transitively) — Fires on each failed sub-check. Behaviour in release builds depends on the project's assert configuration; callers should not assume it throws or aborts in all build types.
- `LOG_CORE_ERROR` / `LOG_APP_ERROR` — Structured logging macros. Hard validation failures use `LOG_CORE_ERROR`; soft/auto-corrected field issues use `LOG_APP_ERROR`. Both write to `log/log.txt` as referenced in the `stderr` hint.
- `CurlMultiDispatcher` — Not called here directly, but the comment inside `Check` explicitly notes that the `m_MaxInflightAiCalls` cap is a local resource guard only; the real back-pressure against HTTP 429 responses lives in `CurlMultiDispatcher`.

**Threading / lifetime:**

`ConfigChecker` holds no heap resources and has trivial construction and destruction. It is not thread-safe: `m_ConfigIsOk` is a plain `bool` written by `Check` and read by `ConfigIsOk`; concurrent calls from multiple threads without external synchronisation are a data race. In practice `Check` is expected to be called once, synchronously, during engine startup before any worker threads are spawned, so this is not a concern in the normal usage pattern. The class owns nothing; `EngineConfig` is passed by reference and must outlive the call.

**Notes:**

- Auto-correction of optional fields only happens when all three hard checks pass. If any path or API check fails, the numeric fields are left as-is and the engine will not start with corrected values — the corrective `LOG_APP_ERROR` messages are therefore never emitted alongside a failed startup.
- The URL check requires the literal substring `"https://"` to be present and the total URL length to exceed 8 characters. Plain HTTP URLs, bare hostnames, or empty strings all fail silently via `CORE_ASSERT` before returning `false`.
- The API interface bounds check uses `<` not `<=`: `apiInterfaces.size() < apiIndex` means an `apiIndex` exactly equal to `size()` (one past the end) passes the bounds check but would produce undefined behaviour on the subsequent array access. This is a latent off-by-one; callers should ensure `m_ApiIndex` is a valid zero-based index.
- `m_MaxInflightAiCalls` is clamped to `1000` when zero or above `10000`; it is not clamped for values in `[1, 10000]`. The engine treats `0` as invalid (no AI calls permitted).
- `m_PythonEngines` is clamped to `4` when zero or above `16`; the comment implies this controls how many embedded Python interpreter instances are allocated.
- Port validation is explicitly skipped here with a comment pointing to `configParser.cpp` as the authoritative location for that check.

### 126_doc_engine_keys_credential_h

<a id="126-doc-engine-keys-credential-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/126_doc_engine_keys_credential_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH Hardcoded Secrets and Insecure Storage
- **Location:** Classes ApiKeyCredential, OAuthCredential, KeyPairCredential, BasicAuthCredential (lines ~19–63)
- **Issue:** Secrets such as API keys, OAuth tokens, private keys, and passwords are stored as plain std::string members. There is no evidence of secure memory management (e.g., zeroization on destruction), nor any encryption at rest or in memory. Storing sensitive values directly in heap-allocated std::string may leave secrets in memory after use, subject to swapping, core dumps, or leaks.
- **Impact:** Secrets may be exposed through memory dumps, swap files, or process inspection, increasing risk of credential theft.
- **Fix:** Use a secure memory container that zeroizes memory upon release for all secrets (e.g., a custom secure_string or OS-provided facilities). Consider encrypting credentials at rest, and avoid unnecessary long-lived secrets in memory.

### MEDIUM - Potential Secret Leakage via Serialization or Logging
- **Location:** ICredential and subclasses (lines ~19–63)
- **Issue:** All credential fields are public or accessible from outside, and GetType is intended for serialization. If subclasses are serialized or logged directly, secrets may be exposed. The code gives no indication of redaction or filtering during logging/serialization.
- **Impact:** API keys, passwords, and private keys could be leaked in logs, error outputs, or when debugging.
- **Fix:** Implement secret redaction and avoid including sensitive fields in serialization or logging routines. Specifically, do not serialize or log sensitive fields unless explicitly required, and document/redact accordingly.

### LOW - No Integrity/Authentication Mechanism for Credentials
- **Location:** All credential classes
- **Issue:** The interface does not specify any means of validating, checking integrity, or authenticating that the stored credential is correct, unaltered, or associated with a specific user/session.
- **Impact:** Credentials may be tampered with in memory if an attacker achieves code execution in the process, or during (de)serialization if used elsewhere.
- **Fix:** Consider tighter access controls, and use structures that support memory authentication where necessary.

---

If this file is used strictly as a data definition layer and not responsible for transport/serialization/deserialization or logging, these issues may be mitigated elsewhere. However, these design choices are inherently risky for secret handling.


---

### 127_doc_engine_json_configParser_h

<a id="127-doc-engine-json-configparser-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/127_doc_engine_json_configParser_h/docu.output.md`



## ConfigParser

**Role:** `ConfigParser` is the sole reader of `config.json` for the j9t workflow engine. It loads the file with simdjson, validates field presence and types, and fills a fully self-contained `EngineConfig` value object that every other engine subsystem consumes at startup. The class also owns the canonical model-name-to-context-window fallback table and the `ApiInterface`-name auto-generation logic, making it the single source of truth for how raw JSON text becomes the typed configuration that drives thread pools, AI provider routing, rate-limit tuning, request budgets, TLS, MCP, Python engine sizing, and HTTP server parameters.

---

**Public API:**

- `ConfigParser(std::string const& filepathAndFilename) → void` — Stores the config file path and sets `m_State` to `Undefined`. Does no I/O. The path must survive only until `Parse` returns; it is copied internally.

- `GetState() → ConfigParser::State` — Returns the current parse state (`Undefined`, `ConfigOk`, `ParseFailure`, `FileNotFound`, `FileFormatFailure`). Valid to call before `Parse`; will return `Undefined` until `Parse` has run.

- `Parse(EngineConfig& engineConfig) → ConfigParser::State` — The primary entry point. Resets `engineConfig` to its default-constructed state on every call, then performs the full parse. Uses `EngineCore::FileExists` and `EngineCore::IsDirectory` before touching simdjson. On success the method populates every field in `EngineConfig` that is present in the JSON and returns `ConfigOk`. On failure it returns one of `ParseFailure`, `FileNotFound`, or `FileFormatFailure` and leaves `engineConfig` partially or fully zeroed. **Minimum validity check:** the JSON must contain at least one `"queue folder"` key and at least one interface entry with a `"url"` field; anything less yields `FileFormatFailure`. Unknown top-level JSON keys are logged at INFO rather than causing an error.

- `ConfigParsed() → bool` — Convenience predicate; returns `true` only when `m_State == ConfigOk`.

- `EngineConfig::GenerateInterfaceName(url, model, apiType) → std::string` — Static. Extracts the domain portion from a URL (strips scheme and path) and concatenates `domain/model/apiType`. Falls back to `"unknown"` if the URL contains no scheme separator. Called automatically by `ParseInterfaces` when an interface entry omits the `"name"` field.

- `EngineConfig::ResolveMaxContextTokensFromModel(modelName) → uint64_t` — Static. Case-insensitive substring match against a built-in priority-ordered table (gpt-5 → 256 K, gpt-4/gpt-4o/gpt-4-turbo → 128 K, claude → 200 K, gemini-1.5/2 → 1 M, llama/qwen/deepseek/phi → 128 K, mixtral/mistral → 32 K). Returns `kUnknownModelFallbackTokens` (50 000) for anything that does not match. Exposed publicly so runtime-added interfaces created outside the config file can apply the same defaults.

---

**Private API (relevant to maintainers):**

- `ParseInterfaces(simdjson::ondemand::array, EngineConfig&, FieldOccurances&) → void` — Iterates the `"API interfaces"` JSON array. For each element it builds an `ApiInterface`, resolves all optional sub-objects (`rate_limit`, `request_budget`), auto-generates the name if absent, and resolves `m_MaxContextTokens` from the model-name table when the config does not set `max_context_tokens` explicitly. Parsed interfaces are `push_back`-ed into `engineConfig.m_ApiInterfaces` in document order, which determines the meaning of `m_ApiIndex` and `m_JcwfAiInterfaceIndex`.

---

**Collaborators:**

- **simdjson (`simdjson::ondemand`)** — All JSON parsing. The `ondemand` API is used in strict document-traversal order; revisiting fields or out-of-order access will produce simdjson errors. `padded_string::load` performs the file read inside simdjson.
- **`EngineCore::FileExists` / `EngineCore::IsDirectory`** (from `auxiliary/file.h`) — Pre-flight checks before simdjson touches the file.
- **`engine.h`** — Provides `LOG_CORE_INFO`, `LOG_CORE_WARN`, `LOG_CORE_ERROR`, `CORE_ASSERT`, and `CORE_HARD_STOP` macros used throughout parsing.
- **`AiRequestPool`** — Consumes `RateLimit` and `RequestBudget` fields at submit time to compute `CURLOPT_TIMEOUT_MS`. The budget formula is: `(inputTokens/1000 × m_Per1kInputTokenSeconds + outputTokens/1000 × m_Per1kOutputTokenSeconds + m_FixedOverheadSeconds) × m_SafetyMarginFactor`, clamped to `[m_MinSeconds, m_MaxSeconds]`.
- **Downstream engine subsystems** — Thread pool sizing (`m_MaxThreads`), Python pool (`m_PythonEngines`), queue watcher (`m_QueueFolderFilepath`, `m_MaxFileSizekB`), workflow runner (`m_WorkflowsFolderFilepath`, `m_JcwfBatchSize`), HTTP server (`m_Port`, `m_TlsCert`, `m_TlsKey`, `m_TrustedProxyHeader`, `m_TrustedRoleHeader`, `m_MaxRequestBodyMB`), secrets loader (`m_KeysFilePath`, `m_McpKeysFilePath`), and session manager (`m_SessionTimeoutHours`) all read directly from the populated `EngineConfig` struct.

---

**Threading / lifetime:**

`ConfigParser` carries no internal synchronization. It is intended to be constructed and used on a single thread during engine startup before worker threads are launched. The `EngineConfig` output is a plain value type with no heap-allocated shared state; once `Parse` returns, the caller owns the struct entirely and may pass it freely to other threads. `ConfigParser` itself may be discarded after `Parse` returns — it holds only the file path string and the state enum. Calling `Parse` a second time on the same instance is safe but unconventional; it will reset `engineConfig` and re-read the file from disk.

---

**Notes:**

- **`CORE_ASSERT` on type mismatches is a hard abort in debug builds.** If a JSON field has the wrong type (e.g., `"verbose"` is a number instead of a boolean), the assert fires before any error path runs. In release builds the assert is typically compiled away, so the subsequent simdjson call may silently produce a default value or an unhandled error code.
- **`CORE_HARD_STOP` on an unrecognised `"API"` string is unconditional.** Any interface entry whose `"API"` value is not one of `API1`–`API6` or `Test` terminates the process regardless of build type.
- **Minimum validity threshold is deliberately lax.** `ConfigOk` requires only that `"queue folder"` appeared at least once and that at least one interface's `"url"` was parsed (tracked via the shared `FieldOccurances` array). All other fields are optional and silently default.
- **`m_ApiIndex` and `m_JcwfAiInterfaceIndex` are not bounds-checked during parsing.** Callers are responsible for validating these indices against `m_ApiInterfaces.size()` before use. `m_JcwfAiInterfaceIndex == -1` means "use `m_ApiIndex`".
- **`max_context_tokens == 0` in the config triggers the fallback table; setting it explicitly to `0` in JSON is therefore indistinguishable from omitting it.** Use any positive value to override.
- **`session_timeout_hours` is clamped to `[1, 168]` (one week) with a warning log.** Values outside that range are silently replaced with `8`.
- **`port == 0` means auto-select**: callers should interpret `0` as "choose 8080 for plain HTTP or 8443 for HTTPS based on whether `m_TlsCert` and `m_TlsKey` are set."
- **Interface order in `m_ApiInterfaces` matches document order in the JSON array.** The numeric `m_ApiIndex` and `m_JcwfAiInterfaceIndex` are positional indices into this vector, so reordering entries in `config.json` changes which provider is selected.
- **`use_bash` is parsed on all platforms** but has effect only on Windows; a platform-conditional info log makes the no-op explicit.
- **The `FieldOccurances` array is shared between `Parse` and `ParseInterfaces`.** The `Url`, `Model`, `InterfaceType`, `InterfaceName`, `InterfaceDescription`, and `InterfaceKeyName` counters accumulate across all interface entries, not per-interface, so an occurrence count greater than one for those fields simply reflects multiple interface definitions.


---

### 127_doc_engine_keys_jwtGenerator_h

<a id="127-doc-engine-keys-jwtgenerator-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/127_doc_engine_keys_jwtGenerator_h/docu.output.md`



### MEDIUM Improper Handling of Private Key Material in Memory
- **Location:** JwtGenerator::Generate (approx. lines 41–102), ComputePublicKeyFingerprint (approx. lines 103–146)
- **Issue:** Private key PEM and EVP_PKEY structures are used and then freed, but the memory is not securely zeroed out after use. The PEM string and private key structure may remain in memory after use, especially “privateKeyPem” string, which can be paged out or leaked through later heap reuse.
- **Impact:** In case of memory disclosure vulnerabilities elsewhere, secrets may be recovered from memory, increasing risk of compromise of all tokens and signed data.
- **Fix:** Overwrite (zero out) the memory backing privateKeyPem and any sensitive buffers after use. For std::string, use e.g., `std::fill(privateKeyPem.begin(), privateKeyPem.end(), 0);` after the key is no longer needed.

### MEDIUM Potential Injection via Unsanitized JWT Payload Construction
- **Location:** JwtGenerator::GenerateSnowflakeJwt (approx. lines 147–198)
- **Issue:** The payload and header JSON are constructed via string concatenation without any escaping or input validation, e.g. for qualifiedUser, fingerprint, account, user. If these input values contain special characters (such as double quotes or backslashes), this could break JSON format, leading to malformed JWTs or in some contexts, code injection into downstream consumers.
- **Impact:** An attacker controlling 'account' or 'user' could inject arbitrary content into JWT claims, possibly manipulating allowed claims or interfering with downstream JWT parsing logic.
- **Fix:** Ensure values inserted into JSON (issuer, subject, fingerprint) are properly JSON-escaped. Use a safe JSON builder/library rather than string concatenation for payloads.

### LOW Use of Custom Base64 Implementation Without Constant-Time Guarantees
- **Location:** JwtGenerator::Base64UrlEncode and in ComputePublicKeyFingerprint (lines approx. 20–63, 110–140)
- **Issue:** Custom Base64 encoding routines are used. These may have subtle bugs or timing variations, which in rare cases could be leveraged in extremely sensitive cryptographic code.
- **Impact:** Likelihood is low, but using non-hardened encoding could enable very advanced timing attacks against cryptographic operations.
- **Fix:** If available, use Base64 encode routines from OpenSSL or another well-vetted constant-time library.

### LOW JWT Output Treated As Secret
- **Location:** JwtGenerator::Generate, near end (approx. line 90)
- **Issue:** The resulting JWT is registered as a secret in SecretRedactor (SecretRedactor::Get().AddSecret(jwt);), but the privateKeyPem and any failures during processing are not similarly treated.
- **Impact:** If error messages or logs are not consistently redacted, secrets like the private key PEM may be leaked to logs. The JWT itself as a secret is a good step, but full coverage should be verified elsewhere.
- **Fix:** Ensure all error handling/context that might contain elements of privateKeyPem are properly redacted throughout the application, not just the resulting token.

---

No CRITICAL or HIGH issues were found in this file, but issues above should be addressed for higher assurance.


---

### 128_doc_engine_json_jsonHelper_h

<a id="128-doc-engine-json-jsonhelper-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/128_doc_engine_json_jsonHelper_h/docu.output.md`



## JsonHelper

**Role:** `JsonHelper` is a small, stateless utility class within the `AIAssistant` namespace that escapes raw string content so it can be safely embedded inside a JSON string value. It exists to prevent malformed JSON when user-supplied text, AI model output, or other dynamic content is interpolated directly into JSON payloads before being sent to external providers or webhooks. It does not perform full JSON serialisation; its sole job is character-level escaping of a single string field's content.

**Public API:**

- `SanitizeForJson(const std::string& input) → std::string` — Iterates over every character in `input` and produces an escaped copy suitable for placement between JSON double-quote delimiters. The following transformations are applied:
  - `"` → `\"`
  - `\` → `\\`
  - `\n` → `\n` (literal two-character escape sequence)
  - `\r` → `\r`
  - `\t` → `\t`
  - The null character (`\0`) is **silently dropped** from the output rather than escaped or propagated.
  - All other characters are copied verbatim.

  The output string has capacity pre-reserved to `input.size()`, so it allocates at most one heap buffer in the common case where no escaping expands the string. The function is purely computational — no I/O, no state mutation, no exceptions thrown.

**Collaborators:** Depends only on `json/jsonHelper.h`, which in turn includes `engine.h`. The standard library (`std::string`) is the only runtime dependency. Callers elsewhere in the engine are expected to be the sites that construct JSON request bodies for AI provider calls, webhook payloads, or any other place where untrusted or user-generated text is embedded into a JSON string literal.

**Threading / lifetime:** `JsonHelper` has no member state whatsoever — the default constructor and destructor are both trivial. Instances are freely copyable, stack-allocatable, and safe to use concurrently from multiple threads without synchronisation. There is no shared mutable state and no dependency on any external object's lifetime.

**Notes:**

- The null character (`\0`) is stripped silently rather than being converted to `\u0000`. Callers processing binary data or strings that legitimately contain embedded nulls will lose those characters without any error or indication. This is the one substantively non-obvious behaviour.
- The function does **not** escape Unicode non-ASCII characters, control characters outside the four handled cases (e.g., `\b` → `\u0008`, `\f` → `\u000C`, or other C0 controls), or characters above U+001F that certain strict JSON validators require to be escaped. Content containing such characters will pass through unmodified; whether that is acceptable depends on the receiving endpoint's JSON parser.
- The function does not wrap the result in double quotes. The caller is responsible for placing the returned string between `"` delimiters in the final JSON document.
- Because only a fixed set of single characters can expand (at most doubling in length to two characters), the worst-case output size is `2 × input.size()`. The `reserve(input.size())` call is a lower-bound hint, not a guarantee of no reallocation.


---

### 128_doc_engine_keys_keyEncryption_h

<a id="128-doc-engine-keys-keyencryption-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/128_doc_engine_keys_keyEncryption_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Unintentional Secret Leakage via Logging
- **Location:** KeyEncryption::{Encrypt,Decrypt} (all error returns, lines ~33–191)
- **Issue:** Error logs in Encrypt/Decrypt may include user-controlled input sizes or version bytes in log messages. If LOG_CORE_ERROR is not properly sanitized or is configured to log values/inputs, this may leak info about the use of the API, and in some callsites, sensitive lengths or version bytes.
- **Impact:** In a verbose logging environment, an operational error could potentially leak input sizes, magic/version info, or error context, which — especially in aggregate or with poor logging discipline — could facilitate later attacks or leak metadata about encrypted content.
- **Fix:** Ensure LOG_CORE_ERROR never records plaintext, password, keys, sensitive buffer contents, or detailed accidental input content. Avoid logging untrusted input or make logging functions aware/safe for secret context. Review all LOG_CORE_ERROR usage for context sanitization.

---

### NONE
No other security issues identified.
