### 129_doc_engine_keys_credential_h

<a id="129-doc-engine-keys-credential-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/129_doc_engine_keys_credential_h/docu.output.md`



## ICredential Hierarchy (`ICredential.h` / `AIAssistant` namespace)

**Role:** Defines the abstract base class `ICredential` and four concrete credential subtypes used throughout the j9t workflow engine to represent provider authentication material in a type-safe, polymorphic hierarchy. This replaces the earlier flat `ProviderConfig::m_ApiKey` string field and gives `KeyManager` a uniform storage and dispatch mechanism for API keys, OAuth 2.0 tokens, RSA key pairs, and username/password pairs across all supported integrations (AI providers, databases, webhooks, etc.).

---

**Public API:**

- `ICredential::GetType() const → std::string` — Pure virtual discriminator returning a stable string tag used during serialization and deserialization. Must be implemented by every concrete subtype. Do not rely on `typeid` or `dynamic_cast` for dispatch; use this tag instead.

- `ICredential::m_Name` — Non-const public data member holding the credential's unique logical name, which is expected to match the corresponding key name registered in `KeyManager`. Callers setting this must ensure it is unique within the manager's namespace; duplicates will silently shadow or overwrite depending on `KeyManager`'s insertion policy.

- `ApiKeyCredential::GetType() → "api_key"` — Represents a simple opaque API key or personal access token stored in `m_ApiKey`. No expiry semantics; rotation must be handled externally.

- `OAuthCredential::GetType() → "oauth"` — Holds a full OAuth 2.0 token set: `m_AccessToken`, `m_RefreshToken`, `m_ExpiresAt` (Unix epoch seconds, `int64_t`), and `m_Scopes`. Callers are responsible for checking `m_ExpiresAt` and invoking the refresh flow before use; this class carries no auto-refresh logic.

- `KeyPairCredential::GetType() → "key_pair"` — Stores an RSA private key in PEM format in `m_PrivateKeyPem`. Documented primary use case is Snowflake JWT authentication. The corresponding public key is not stored here; it is assumed to be pre-registered with the remote service.

- `BasicAuthCredential::GetType() → "credentials"` — Holds `m_Username` and `m_Password` for systems that accept HTTP Basic Auth or equivalent (e.g., PostgreSQL, SMTP). Note the tag string is `"credentials"`, not `"basic_auth"` — serialization code must use this exact value.

---

**Collaborators:**

- **`KeyManager`** — Primary consumer. Stores and retrieves `std::unique_ptr<ICredential>` (or shared equivalents) keyed by `m_Name`. The credential type tag from `GetType()` drives serialization to/from whatever persistence format `KeyManager` uses.
- **`ProviderConfig`** — Predecessor design that held a flat `m_ApiKey` string; this hierarchy supersedes it. Code still referencing `ProviderConfig::m_ApiKey` should be considered a migration candidate.
- **Signing / auth middleware** — Components that perform JWT signing (Snowflake), OAuth header injection, or Basic Auth header construction will `dynamic_cast` or pattern-match on `GetType()` to extract the right fields.

---

**Threading / lifetime:**

- All credential objects are intended to be owned by `KeyManager` via `std::unique_ptr<ICredential>` or similar smart pointer. Callers must not retain raw pointers to credential objects across `KeyManager` mutation operations (insertion, deletion, reload).
- No internal synchronization is provided. If `KeyManager` can be accessed from multiple threads, all reads and writes to credential objects must be externally synchronized.
- `OAuthCredential::m_AccessToken` and `m_ExpiresAt` may be written by a token-refresh path concurrently with reads by a request-dispatch path; the caller owning that refresh logic must apply a lock before mutating these fields.

---

**Notes:**

- The string returned by `GetType()` on `BasicAuthCredential` is `"credentials"`, not `"basic_auth"`. Any serialization round-trip or switch/map keyed on type strings must use this exact value or deserialization will silently produce the wrong concrete type.
- `OAuthCredential::m_ExpiresAt` is zero-initialized (`int64_t{0}`), which evaluates to 1 January 1970 UTC. Code that checks token validity must treat `0` as "never set / always expired" rather than a valid future timestamp.
- There is no `Clone()` or copy-factory method on `ICredential`. Deep-copying credentials requires knowing the concrete type; callers that need to snapshot credential state must either add a virtual clone method or use `GetType()` to dispatch a manual copy.
- `m_Name` and all token/key fields are plain `std::string` with no in-memory encryption. Credential objects should not be written to logs or serialized to untrusted sinks without scrubbing sensitive fields first.


---

### 129_doc_engine_keys_keyManager_h

<a id="129-doc-engine-keys-keymanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/129_doc_engine_keys_keyManager_h/docu.output.md`



### [HIGH] Insecure Storage/Exposure of Secrets in Memory
- **Location:** AIAssistant::KeyManager (multiple functions; struct ProviderConfig; lines ~17+, 114+, 360+)
- **Issue:** Sensitive values such as API keys, passwords, client secrets, and private keys are stored directly in std::string fields of ProviderConfig and handled as std::string throughout the codebase except for the cached master password (which uses SecureString). These secrets are thus subject to lingering in memory, paging to disk, or accidental logging.
- **Impact:** Secrets in std::string may be inadvertently exposed (e.g., through core dumps, memory analysis, or by being written into logs). Attackers with access to process memory or memory dumps could extract credentials.
- **Fix:** Use a SecureString (or equivalent explicit zeroing secure container) for all secret material fields (m_ApiKey, m_ClientSecret, m_Password, m_RefreshToken, m_PrivateKeyPem, and any sensitive values in m_Params) and ensure all manipulations are done securely, avoiding copies to std::string where possible.

---

### [MEDIUM] Potential Secrets Exposure via Logs
- **Location:** ProviderConfig population and logging in functions like Load, Save, LoadPlaintext, SavePlaintext, LoadFromEnvironment, ParseProvidersJson (multiple, especially lines ~116, ~149, ~176, ~184, ~469)
- **Issue:** Some log messages include counts and descriptive names that could (depending on log config elsewhere) leak provider names or inadvertently leak sensitive details if logging is extended or misused in future. While SecretRedactor support is shown for AWS material, there is no redaction mechanism demonstrated for other secret fields when logging provider data, usernames, or unexpected exception messages. "LOG_CORE_ERROR/WARN/INFO" is called directly with provider file paths and names, which may have been attacker-supplied.
- **Impact:** Logs could leak sensitive paths or secrets if log statements are altered or provider names are attacker-controlled, especially given the generic logging interface.
- **Fix:** Audit all logging to ensure that only non-sensitive details are included. Use a redactor for all secret fields. Never log plaintext secrets or values derived from attacker input. Sanitize file paths and provider names if included in logs.

---

### [MEDIUM] Insufficient Input Sanitization in SerializeToJson
- **Location:** SerializeToJson (lines ~413+)
- **Issue:** SerializeToJson hand-builds JSON using unescaped strings for provider names, display names, endpoints, and most other string fields except for "params" and a few key fields. The escape function is applied only for m_Params but not for other string values, leaving them susceptible to broken JSON output, or injection when deserialized elsewhere.
- **Impact:** If a provider name or field contains a quote, newline, or control character, this could result in invalid JSON or opportunities to inject arbitrary fields, impacting downstream consumers or data integrity.
- **Fix:** Apply proper JSON escaping for all output values in SerializeToJson, not just m_Params and PEM keys. Do not trust that provider names will always be "controlled strings" — defensive encode everything.

---

### [MEDIUM] Unvalidated/Unrestricted File Path Access
- **Location:** KeyManager::Load, Save, LoadPlaintext, SavePlaintext, Unlock (lines ~114, ~149, ~176, ~184, ~204)
- **Issue:** All file operations accept arbitrary std::filesystem::path, with no validation (e.g., against traversal, symlink misdirection, or writing to system paths) and no file permission management (especially on "plaintext" operations).
- **Impact:** An attacker able to specify or influence the keysFilePath value could read/write/overwrite arbitrary files on disk, leading to denial of service or persistence attacks. Saving plaintext secrets to insecure locations (with insufficient file permissions) is a risk.
- **Fix:** Validate input file paths are within allowed directories and not symlinks. Set restrictive file permissions (0600 for Unix) when writing secrets, especially in SavePlaintext. Refuse to operate on "plaintext" files in production or non-dev environments. Consider file locking where race conditions are possible.

---

### [LOW] Limited Race Condition Protection on Key File Operations
- **Location:** KeyManager::Save, Load, SavePlaintext, LoadPlaintext, Unlock (lines ~114, ~149, ~176, ~184, ~204)
- **Issue:** No explicit file locking or atomicity guarantees for secrets file reads and writes, nor protection from TOCTOU attacks between existence check and open. If multiple processes attempt to write/read at the same time, this may cause inconsistent or corrupted state.
- **Impact:** Possible secrets file corruption or disclosure if a file is manipulated concurrently, or if an attacker can swap out files between checks and accesses.
- **Fix:** Use atomic file write patterns (write to temp file and rename), employ file locks for exclusive access, and re-check file state after open. 

---

### [LOW] No Password Strength/Complexity Checking for Master Password
- **Location:** KeyManager::Load, Unlock (lines ~114, ~204)
- **Issue:** No enforcement that the "masterPassword" used to encrypt secrets is of sufficient strength or entropy.
- **Impact:** Users could use weak or default passwords, making encrypted files vulnerable to offline brute-force attacks.
- **Fix:** Check password length/complexity on Load/Unlock and issue warnings or refuse operation for weak passwords.

---

### [LOW] Storing Master Password in Memory for Extended Periods
- **Location:** KeyManager::m_CachedMasterPassword, .GetCachedMasterPassword (lines ~43, ~114, ~126)
- **Issue:** The master password is cached after a successful unlock. This is necessary for some operations but increases memory exposure window.
- **Impact:** Compromise of process memory exposes the master password for longer than strictly necessary.
- **Fix:** Where feasible, clear the cached password after it's no longer needed, or at least on user logout/server stop.

---

### [LOW] No Validation on Provider Name or Parameters
- **Location:** AddProvider, UpdateProvider, RemoveProvider (lines ~287, ~298, ~311)
- **Issue:** Provider names and parameter keys/values are added with no normalization or disallowed character checks.
- **Impact:** Malicious input (e.g., provider names with control characters) could result in broken registry state or log injection.
- **Fix:** Validate provider names and parameters for allowed character sets and length.

---

### [LOW] No Limits on Number or Size of Providers/Fields
- **Location:** ParseProvidersJson (line ~362), AddProvider, etc.
- **Issue:** No upper bound on the number of providers, number of params, or field sizes (except for a minimal check on OPENAI_API_KEY).
- **Impact:** This may allow DoS by huge provider blobs or fields.
- **Fix:** Enforce reasonable size and count limits during parsing and deserialization.

---

### [LOW] Lack of Error Handling for SecureString::Get (Potential for Leaks)
- **Location:** GetCachedMasterPassword (line ~126)
- **Issue:** Returns std::string from SecureString::Get may accidentally make (temporarily) plaintext copies in memory on the heap and stack.
- **Impact:** Minor risk, since caller may not handle further clearing.
- **Fix:** Document and warn users/callers of this function to treat results as sensitive and clear memory after use.

---

### [LOW] No Integrity Validation on Decrypted JSON Secrets
- **Location:** Load (line ~114), ParseProvidersJson (line ~362)
- **Issue:** Decrypted JSON is parsed directly after decryption without an integrity or authenticity check (HMAC, signature, etc.), relying entirely on the encryption ciphers used in KeyEncryption.
- **Impact:** If encrypted file is tampered with and decryption doesn't fail, there's a risk of silent corruption or injection of attacker data.
- **Fix:** Use authenticated encryption (e.g., AES-GCM) or a separate MAC to check authenticity/integrity, and fail if the check fails.

---

**Note:** This review is based only on the code shown; the underlying implementations of KeyEncryption, SecureString, and SecretRedactor are not visible. Therefore, cryptographic safety there cannot be evaluated.

---

If further controls already exist (e.g., full secure logging or encryption with integrity in KeyEncryption), some LOW/MEDIUM findings may be mitigated. However, based strictly on this code, the above issues apply.


---

### 12_doc_application_cloud_cloudConnectionManager_h

<a id="12-doc-application-cloud-cloudconnectionmanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/12_doc_application_cloud_cloudConnectionManager_h/docu.output.md`



## CloudConnectionManager

**Role:** `CloudConnectionManager` is the authoritative in-memory registry for `CloudConnection` configuration objects within the j9t workflow engine. It stores named connection descriptors — each carrying a type, endpoint URL, associated secret key name, authentication type, and an arbitrary string-to-string parameter map — and provides fully thread-safe CRUD access to them. The class owns the serialization round-trip to and from the `config.json` format used by the REST API save endpoint: `ParseConnectionsJson` populates the store from a JSON string, and `SerializeToJson` produces the canonical on-disk representation. A dirty flag tracks whether in-memory state has diverged from what was last persisted, allowing callers to decide when a write-back is needed.

---

**Public API:**

- `AddConnection(CloudConnection connection) → bool` — Inserts a new entry keyed by `connection.m_Name`. Returns `false` and logs a warning if `m_Name` is empty or a connection with that name already exists; returns `true` and sets the dirty flag on success. Takes `connection` by value; the object is moved into the internal map.

- `UpdateConnection(std::string const& name, CloudConnection connection) → bool` — Replaces the existing entry identified by `name` with the supplied `connection`. Forcibly sets `connection.m_Name = name` before storing, ensuring the key and the struct's own name field stay consistent. Returns `false` with a warning if `name` is not found. Sets the dirty flag on success.

- `RemoveConnection(std::string const& name) → bool` — Erases the entry for `name`. Returns `false` with a warning if not found. Sets the dirty flag on success.

- `GetConnection(std::string const& name) const → CloudConnection const*` — Returns a raw pointer into the internal map for the named entry, or `nullptr` if absent. See the threading section for the critical lifetime caveat on this pointer.

- `GetConnectionNames() const → std::vector<std::string>` — Returns a snapshot copy of all registered names. Order is unspecified (hash map iteration order).

- `GetAllConnections() const → std::vector<CloudConnection>` — Returns a snapshot copy of all `CloudConnection` objects. Also unordered.

- `HasConnections() const → bool` — Returns `true` if at least one connection is registered.

- `IsDirty() const → bool` — Returns `true` when in-memory state has been mutated since the last `ClearDirty()` call. Not mutex-protected; intended for single-threaded persistence coordination.

- `ClearDirty()` — Resets the dirty flag to `false`. Call after a successful write-back to `config.json`. Not mutex-protected.

- `ParseConnectionsJson(std::string const& json) → bool` — Clears the entire existing store and repopulates it from a JSON string. Expects the top-level object to contain a `"connections"` array; each element must be an object with string fields `"name"`, `"type"`, `"endpoint"`, `"key_name"`, `"auth_type"`, and an optional `"params"` sub-object of string-to-string pairs. Uses simdjson's ondemand parser. Returns `false` and logs an error if parsing fails or the `"connections"` key is missing; individual malformed array elements are silently skipped. Elements with an empty `"name"` field are also silently dropped. Does **not** set the dirty flag.

- `SerializeToJson() const → std::string` — Produces a pretty-printed JSON string encoding the current store under a `"connections"` array key. Fields are serialized in hash map iteration order (non-deterministic across runs). Uses the file-local `JsonEscape` helper to escape `"`, `\`, `\n`, `\r`, and `\t` in all string values. Auth type serialization delegates to `AuthTypeToString` (from `cloudConnector.h`). Does **not** set or clear the dirty flag.

---

**Collaborators:**

- `CloudConnection` (`cloud/cloudConnector.h`) — The data struct being managed. Contains public fields `m_Name`, `m_Type`, `m_Endpoint`, `m_KeyName`, `m_AuthType`, and `m_Params` (`std::unordered_map<std::string, std::string>`). `StringToAuthType` and `AuthTypeToString` free functions from the same header handle auth-type enum conversion.

- `engine.h` — Pulled in for the `LOG_CORE_WARN` and `LOG_CORE_ERROR` logging macros used throughout the implementation.

- `simdjson` (ondemand API) — Used exclusively inside `ParseConnectionsJson` for JSON deserialization. The padded-string requirement is handled internally; callers supply a plain `std::string`.

- REST API / persistence layer (external) — Calls `ParseConnectionsJson` on startup load and `SerializeToJson` + `ClearDirty` when flushing to `config.json`. This manager does not perform I/O itself.

---

**Threading / lifetime:**

All CRUD and read methods acquire `m_Mutex` (`std::shared_mutex`) appropriately: mutating operations (`Add`, `Update`, `Remove`, `ParseConnectionsJson`) take a `std::unique_lock`; read operations (`GetConnection`, `GetConnectionNames`, `GetAllConnections`, `HasConnections`) take a `std::shared_lock`. `SerializeToJson` holds a shared lock for its full iteration.

**Critical:** `GetConnection` returns a raw pointer directly into `m_Connections`. That pointer is only valid for the duration of the caller's own shared lock, which is released before the method returns. Callers must not dereference the returned pointer after any subsequent mutation (including from another thread). For safe long-lived access, copy the returned struct rather than holding the pointer.

`IsDirty` and `ClearDirty` are not mutex-protected and are not safe for concurrent use. The expectation is that the persistence layer coordinates these calls from a single thread or under its own external lock.

`CloudConnectionManager` has a default constructor and is not copyable or movable in any special way; it should be owned as a singleton or stable heap object (raw or `unique_ptr`) so that pointers returned by `GetConnection` remain address-stable.

---

**Notes:**

- `ParseConnectionsJson` performs a full `clear()` of `m_Connections` before populating, meaning a parse failure after the clear leaves the manager empty. Callers should treat a `false` return as a fatal configuration error and avoid continued operation with an empty store.

- The dirty flag is not set by `ParseConnectionsJson`. This matches the intent that loading from disk is not itself a "dirty" state. Callers must not assume a freshly parsed manager needs to be written back.

- `SerializeToJson` output order is hash-map-dependent and will vary between runs, which can produce noisy diffs in `config.json` under version control. If diff stability matters, a sorted serialization pass would need to be added.

- `UpdateConnection` silently overwrites `connection.m_Name` with the lookup key. A caller passing a `CloudConnection` whose `m_Name` differs from `name` will have that name field corrected without any warning.

- Entries with an empty `m_Name` are rejected at `AddConnection` time but are only silently skipped during `ParseConnectionsJson`. There is no schema validation beyond name presence; `m_Type`, `m_Endpoint`, `m_KeyName`, and `m_AuthType` can all be empty strings or their zero values after a parse.


---

### 130_doc_engine_keys_jwtGenerator_h

<a id="130-doc-engine-keys-jwtgenerator-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/130_doc_engine_keys_jwtGenerator_h/docu.output.md`



## JwtGenerator

**Role:** `JwtGenerator` is a stateless utility class inside the `AIAssistant` namespace that produces RS256-signed JSON Web Tokens using OpenSSL's `EVP_DigestSign` API. It covers two usage levels: a generic path that accepts arbitrary header and payload JSON strings, and a Snowflake-specific convenience path that builds the correct header, payload, and public-key fingerprint automatically. The class is the single place in the engine where RSA private keys are loaded and used for JWT signing; any provider that authenticates with RS256 (Snowflake, Google service accounts, etc.) routes through it.

---

**Public API:**

- `Generate(headerJson, payloadJson, privateKeyPem, errorMessage) → std::string` — Takes pre-formed JSON strings for the header and payload plus an RSA private key in PEM text form. Loads the key via `BIO_new_mem_buf` / `PEM_read_bio_PrivateKey`, rejects keys smaller than 2048 bits, builds the signing input as `base64url(header).base64url(payload)`, signs it with `EVP_DigestSignInit` / `EVP_DigestSignUpdate` / `EVP_DigestSignFinal` using SHA-256, and returns the complete `header.payload.signature` JWT string. Returns an empty string on any failure and writes a human-readable reason into `errorMessage`. On success, the resulting JWT is immediately registered with `SecretRedactor::Get().AddSecret()` so it is masked in logs.

- `GenerateSnowflakeJwt(account, user, privateKeyPem, errorMessage) → std::string` — Convenience wrapper that constructs a Snowflake-compliant JWT without the caller having to know the claim structure. It uppercases `account` and `user`, strips any region suffix from the account locator at the first `.` character (e.g. `XY12345.us-east-1` becomes `XY12345`), computes `iat` as the current Unix epoch second via `std::chrono::system_clock`, sets `exp` to `iat + 3600`, derives the public-key fingerprint via `ComputePublicKeyFingerprint`, then assembles `iss`, `sub`, `iat`, and `exp` claims and delegates to `Generate`. The `iss` claim takes the form `ACCOUNT.USER.SHA256:<b64>` and `sub` is `ACCOUNT.USER`.

---

**Collaborators:**

- **OpenSSL** (`openssl/bio.h`, `openssl/evp.h`, `openssl/pem.h`, `openssl/rsa.h`, `openssl/sha.h`) — all cryptographic operations, PEM parsing, DER serialisation, and SHA-256 hashing are performed through OpenSSL's EVP and legacy `SHA256` APIs.
- **`SecretRedactor`** (`log/secretRedactor.h`) — the singleton `SecretRedactor::Get()` is called after every successful `Generate` to register the new JWT as a secret string that must be redacted from log output. Callers do not need to do this themselves.
- **`engine.h`** — included but used only for engine-wide type or macro definitions; no direct calls to engine internals appear in this file.

---

**Threading / lifetime:**

All three public and private methods are `static` and hold no instance state. There are no member variables. Each call allocates and frees its own OpenSSL objects (`BIO`, `EVP_PKEY`, `EVP_MD_CTX`) on the stack/heap within the call frame, so calls are independently re-entrant as long as the OpenSSL library itself has been initialised (standard requirement for any OpenSSL consumer). The one shared side-effect is the call into `SecretRedactor::Get()`; thread-safety of that path depends on `SecretRedactor`'s own locking, not on `JwtGenerator`. No objects of `JwtGenerator` need to be created or destroyed by callers.

---

**Notes:**

- The Base64Url encoding is implemented from scratch rather than using OpenSSL's `BIO_f_base64` filter. Padding `=` characters are stripped and `+`/`/` are replaced with `-`/`_` per RFC 7515. The fingerprint path in `ComputePublicKeyFingerprint`, however, intentionally uses standard Base64 with padding and the `+`/`/` characters intact because Snowflake's `SHA256:` fingerprint format requires non-URL-safe Base64.
- `ComputePublicKeyFingerprint` serialises the public key as a SubjectPublicKeyInfo DER blob via `i2d_PUBKEY` and hashes it with `SHA256`. This matches Snowflake's documented key-pair authentication fingerprint algorithm. It does **not** hash just the key modulus or a different public-key format.
- The Snowflake region-suffix stripping logic is applied only at the first `.` in the uppercased account string. If the account identifier contains no `.`, it is used verbatim as the locator. Callers passing an account that already lacks a region suffix are unaffected.
- If `EVP_PKEY_bits` returns a value below 2048, `Generate` frees the key and returns empty before any signing attempt. The error message includes the actual bit count to aid debugging.
- The raw `privateKeyPem` string is never logged; `EVP_PKEY_free` is called on every exit path, including error paths, so key material is not leaked in OpenSSL's internal structures beyond the call duration.
- `errorMessage` is an out-parameter, not an exception. Callers must check the return value for emptiness and inspect `errorMessage` on failure; no exceptions are thrown.


---

### 130_doc_engine_keys_oauthTokenManager_h

<a id="130-doc-engine-keys-oauthtokenmanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/130_doc_engine_keys_oauthTokenManager_h/docu.output.md`



### HIGH OAuth Refresh Token/Secret Leakage in Logs
- **Location:** Multiple LOG_CORE_ERROR/LOG_CORE_WARN uses, especially in `RefreshToken` (~line 210+)
- **Issue:** OAuth responses, which may contain secrets ("refresh_token", "access_token", error details) are directly logged in error messages (example: `LOG_CORE_ERROR("OAuthTokenManager: refresh for '{}' returned HTTP {} — {}", keyName, httpCode, responseBody...);`). Secret values may also be included in other unexpected error messages or through insufficient redaction.
- **Impact:** Tokens and secrets could be exposed in log files, giving attackers reuse access to OAuth sessions, possible privilege escalation, or broad lateral movement.
- **Fix:** Ensure all log output that includes response bodies or error messages is properly redacted by SecretRedactor before being logged. Sanitize, mask, or omit highly sensitive fields ("access_token", "refresh_token", "client_secret") in logs.

---

### MEDIUM Possible Pre-Authorization of Access to Tokens
- **Location:** OAuthTokenManager::GetAccessToken(), OAuthTokenManager::StoreTokens(), HydrateFromKeyManager() (~line 65, 120, 53)
- **Issue:** No explicit authentication or authorization check is performed before permitting read/write of access or refresh tokens via these API calls. Thus, any code path able to call these functions (directly or indirectly) can obtain valid OAuth tokens and potentially act on behalf of the user or system.
- **Impact:** Attackers or compromised internal code may escalate privilege by retrieving valid credentials used for downstream API access.
- **Fix:** Ensure that access to all OAuth token operations is gated by a strict privilege check and/or access control policy at the API entry points (e.g., only permit roles/accounts authorized to handle OAuth tokens to call these methods).

---

### MEDIUM Insecure Construction of HTTP POST Body (Injection Risk)
- **Location:** OAuthTokenManager::RefreshToken() (~line 162)
- **Issue:** The HTTP POST body for the refresh request is manually constructed by concatenating key/value pairs directly, without URL-encoding the values (`postBody = ... + entry.m_RefreshToken + ...`). If any token, client_id, or secret contains an ampersand, equal sign, percent, or other special character, this could result in malformed requests, or if any fields are attacker-supplied, it could manipulate the POST data structure.
- **Impact:** May allow attacker to inject additional parameters into the POST body, or cause OAuth requests to fail in ways that could be leveraged for DoS or privilege confusion.
- **Fix:** URL-encode all values used in the POST body with a proper encoding function before concatenation. Never concatenate sensitive or user-supplied data in HTTP requests without context-appropriate encoding.

---

### LOW Insufficient TLS Verification on Refresh Requests
- **Location:** OAuthTokenManager::RefreshToken(), cURL options (~line 172-180)
- **Issue:** Only `CURLOPT_CAINFO` is set conditionally based on non-empty `caBundle`, but no explicit cURL option is used to enforce peer/host certificate verification (`CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST`). Defaults may or may not be overridden elsewhere, but this is not explicit.
- **Impact:** If a cURL global or system policy disables verification, attacker could man-in-the-middle the token endpoint and intercept credentials.
- **Fix:** Explicitly set `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYPEER` to `1L` and `CURLOPT_SSL_VERIFYHOST` to `2L` for every cURL context handling OAuth credentials.

---

### LOW Secret Lifetime and Clear Removal (Memory Hygiene)
- **Location:** TokenEntry member management, updates in RefreshToken(), StoreTokens(), etc.
- **Issue:** While SecretRedactor unregisters old tokens (RemoveSecret), the previous string contents are not overwritten (zeroed) before being replaced/deleted, meaning that sensitive tokens may persist in heap memory after replacement for some undefined period.
- **Impact:** Secrets could be read from system memory by malicious code or forensic tools after credential rotation.
- **Fix:** Overwrite (e.g., using `std::fill(entry.m_AccessToken.begin(), entry.m_AccessToken.end(), 0)` before assignment/removal) all string buffers containing secrets before deleting or replacing them in memory.

---

### LOW No HTTP Proxy Restrictions (SSRF Potential)
- **Location:** cURL use in RefreshToken() (~line 172)
- **Issue:** cURL may honor environment proxy variables, allowing OAuthTokenManager (if attacker can induce it to refresh against an arbitrary URL) to send sensitive credentials to attacker-controlled hosts.
- **Impact:** SSRF and credential exfiltration if m_TokenEndpoint is attacker-influenced and cURL proxy is set.
- **Fix:** Explicitly set `CURLOPT_PROXY` to an empty string (or otherwise ensure proxies are not used for sensitive token refreshes), and validate that m_TokenEndpoint only matches trusted domains.

---

### LOW Lack of Resource Boundaries on Token Map
- **Location:** m_Tokens (std::unordered_map), storage via HydrateFromKeyManager/StoreTokens (~line 47, 120)
- **Issue:** No explicit maximum map size or per-user/caller limitation. Untrusted code could bloat the token store with large numbers of keys, leading to memory exhaustion DoS.
- **Impact:** Unbounded memory allocation and risk of denial-of-service.
- **Fix:** Apply reasonable limits on the number of token entries stored per process and/or per trusted identity.

---

### LOW Partial Authentication Error Messages
- **Location:** Error strings/log entries in GetAccessToken(), RefreshToken()
- **Issue:** Detailed error messages include keyName and error context, which could be abused for information leakage if error messages are not properly filtered from user-facing output.
- **Impact:** May leak information about credential existence, expiry, or system configuration.
- **Fix:** Ensure error messages containing internal details or identifiers are only exposed to privileged logs, not to user-facing APIs/services.

---

### LOW Inadequate Validation of JSON Token Responses
- **Location:** OAuthTokenManager::RefreshToken() (~line 206+)
- **Issue:** Only "access_token" is checked for existence; no verification of token type ("token_type" etc.) or structural fields specified in RFC6749, and no sanity check on e.g. string length or character set for token values.
- **Impact:** May amplify vulnerabilities in downstream components trusting unsanitized token data.
- **Fix:** Validate types/fields per the OAuth provider specification, ensure all string values are length/charset checked, and ignore/clean unknown JSON fields.

---

If any of the above issues are mitigated elsewhere (e.g., if cURL defaults are set safely process-wide), those mitigations are not visible in the provided file and should be verified.


---

### 131_doc_engine_keys_keyEncryption_h

<a id="131-doc-engine-keys-keyencryption-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/131_doc_engine_keys_keyEncryption_h/docu.output.md`



## KeyEncryption

**Role:** `KeyEncryption` is a stateless utility class within the `AIAssistant` namespace responsible for encrypting and decrypting secret key material (expected to be JSON strings) using AES-256-GCM authenticated encryption. It derives the symmetric key from a caller-supplied master password via PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 with a randomly generated 16-byte salt per encryption operation. The class defines and enforces a fixed binary blob format identified by the four-byte magic `JKEY` and a version byte (`0x01`), making it the canonical persistence format for protected secrets within the j9t engine's key management subsystem.

---

**Public API:**

- `Encrypt(plaintext, masterPassword) → std::vector<uint8_t>` — Takes a plaintext JSON string and a master password. Generates fresh random salt and IV via `RAND_bytes`, derives a 32-byte AES key using `PKCS5_PBKDF2_HMAC` with SHA-256 and 100,000 iterations, then encrypts via OpenSSL's `EVP_aes_256_gcm()`. Returns the complete self-contained blob: `[4-byte magic][1-byte version][16-byte salt][12-byte IV][ciphertext][16-byte GCM tag]` — total overhead per call is 33 bytes of header plus 16 bytes of tag. Returns an empty vector on any OpenSSL failure (PRNG failure, cipher init failure, etc.); all failures are logged via `LOG_CORE_ERROR`. The derived key is erased from the stack with `OPENSSL_cleanse` before returning.

- `Decrypt(encryptedBlob, masterPassword) → std::string` — Takes a blob previously produced by `Encrypt` and the same master password. Validates the minimum blob size (`HEADER_SIZE + TAG_SIZE` = 49 bytes), checks the `JKEY` magic, and rejects any version byte other than `0x01`. Re-derives the key from the embedded salt using the same PBKDF2 parameters, decrypts, and authenticates the GCM tag via `EVP_DecryptFinal_ex`. Returns the plaintext JSON string on success. Returns an empty string on any failure: wrong password, corrupted data, bad magic, unsupported version, or OpenSSL errors. Authentication failure (wrong password or tampered ciphertext) is the expected path for incorrect passwords and is distinguishable from success only by the empty return value. The derived key is erased with `OPENSSL_cleanse` in all code paths, including all error exits.

---

**Collaborators:**

- **OpenSSL (`libcrypto`)** — All cryptographic operations delegate to OpenSSL's EVP API: `EVP_CIPHER_CTX_new/free`, `EVP_EncryptInit_ex`, `EVP_EncryptUpdate`, `EVP_EncryptFinal_ex`, `EVP_DecryptInit_ex`, `EVP_DecryptUpdate`, `EVP_DecryptFinal_ex`, `EVP_CIPHER_CTX_ctrl` (for IV length, get-tag, and set-tag), `RAND_bytes`, `PKCS5_PBKDF2_HMAC`, and `OPENSSL_cleanse`. The caller's build must link against OpenSSL.
- **`engine.h`** — Pulled in by the `.cpp` for the `LOG_CORE_ERROR` logging macro. All error paths in both methods emit structured log messages through this macro with context-specific detail (byte counts, version numbers, etc.).
- **`keys/keyEncryption.h`** — The header containing the class declaration; included by the `.cpp`. Any other module that reads or writes the `JKEY` blob format must include this header to use `Encrypt`/`Decrypt`.

---

**Threading / lifetime:**

Both methods are `static` and the class holds no instance state whatsoever; there is nothing to construct, destruct, or share. The functions are safe to call concurrently from multiple threads provided the caller does not share the same `plaintext`, `masterPassword`, or `encryptedBlob` objects across threads without external synchronisation. Each invocation allocates its own `EVP_CIPHER_CTX` on the heap and frees it before returning; there is no shared mutable state. Key material allocated on the stack (`key[32]`) is zeroed with `OPENSSL_cleanse` before each return path, including all error exits, so sensitive data does not linger in stack frames.

---

**Notes:**

- **Failure is silent at the API boundary.** Both methods signal all failure modes — wrong password, corrupted blob, PRNG failure, OpenSSL internal errors — by returning an empty vector or empty string. Callers cannot distinguish a decryption authentication failure from an OpenSSL initialisation error without inspecting engine logs. Callers must treat any empty return as a total failure and not attempt to use the result.
- **Salt is per-encryption, not per-password.** Each call to `Encrypt` generates a fresh random salt and IV, so encrypting the same plaintext twice with the same password produces different blobs. This is correct and intentional for forward secrecy of stored key material, but callers must store the entire blob — not just ciphertext — to enable decryption.
- **Version `0x01` is the only supported version.** `Decrypt` hard-rejects any blob whose version byte is not `0x01`. There is no upgrade path or migration logic; any future format change requires a new version byte and corresponding handling code.
- **GCM tag placement.** The 16-byte authentication tag is appended at the end of the blob, after the ciphertext. `Decrypt` locates it by computing `blob.size() - TAG_SIZE`; a blob truncated by even one byte will either fail the minimum-size check or cause tag misalignment and subsequent GCM authentication failure.
- **PBKDF2 cost is synchronous and blocking.** 100,000 SHA-256 iterations on the calling thread. This is intentional hardening against brute-force but means callers on latency-sensitive threads (UI, request-handling loops) should dispatch these calls off-thread.
- **No additional authenticated data (AAD).** The header bytes (magic, version, salt, IV) are not fed into the GCM AAD; only the ciphertext is authenticated. A targeted attacker who can flip header bytes (other than salt/IV, which affect key derivation and decryption directly) without detection is a theoretical concern, though in practice a corrupted header will cause key derivation to produce a wrong key or IV misalignment, resulting in authentication failure anyway.

### 132_doc_engine_keys_keyManager_h

<a id="132-doc-engine-keys-keymanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/132_doc_engine_keys_keyManager_h/docu.output.md`



## KeyManager

**Role:** `KeyManager` is the credential store for all AI provider configurations in j9t. It owns an in-memory registry of `ProviderConfig` entries — each carrying the endpoint URL, API key, default model, API type, and the full range of alternative credential materials (OAuth tokens, PEM private keys, basic-auth username/password, and a free-form string-to-string params map for provider-specific extras such as AWS region and Bedrock session tokens or Azure deployment and API version). The class handles loading from and saving to AES-encrypted on-disk files for production use, or plaintext JSON files for development. A `SecureString`-held cached master password enables server-side OAuth token callbacks to re-encrypt and persist updates without re-prompting the user. A `KeyLoadStatus` enum tracks the state of the last load attempt so callers can distinguish "file missing", "no password supplied", and "wrong password" without catching exceptions.

---

**Public API:**

- `Load(keysFilePath, masterPassword) → bool` — Reads the binary file at `keysFilePath` into memory, passes the raw bytes to `KeyEncryption::Decrypt`, then feeds the resulting JSON string to `ParseProvidersJson`. On success, clears `m_Dirty` and stores `masterPassword` in `m_CachedMasterPassword`. Returns `false` and logs an error if the file cannot be opened, is empty, decryption produces an empty string, or JSON parsing fails. Does **not** update `m_KeyLoadStatus` itself — callers must do that, or use `Unlock`.

- `Save(keysFilePath, masterPassword) → bool` — Serializes the provider registry to JSON under a shared lock, encrypts the result via `KeyEncryption::Encrypt`, and writes the blob to `keysFilePath` (truncating). On success, clears `m_Dirty` and caches the password. The shared lock is released before the file I/O, so the in-memory state can change between serialization and write completion if a writer acquires the lock in that window — this is an accepted race for the current use pattern.

- `LoadPlaintext(keysFilePath) → bool` — Development-only variant of `Load` that reads a raw UTF-8 JSON file with no encryption. Does not touch `m_CachedMasterPassword`. Clears `m_Dirty` on success.

- `SavePlaintext(keysFilePath) → bool` — Development-only variant of `Save` that writes unencrypted JSON. Does not touch `m_CachedMasterPassword`. Clears `m_Dirty` on success.

- `LoadFromEnvironment(endpoint, model, apiType) → bool` — Reads `OPENAI_API_KEY` from the process environment; rejects values shorter than 8 characters. Clears the entire provider registry, then inserts a single `"openai"` provider constructed from the env var plus the supplied endpoint, model, and API type strings. Sets `m_DefaultProviderName = "openai"`. Clears `m_Dirty`. This is the backward-compatibility path for pre-multi-provider deployments.

- `Unlock(masterPassword) → bool` — Runtime credential entry point. Requires `m_KeysFilePath` to have been set previously via `SetKeysFilePath`. Checks file existence, then delegates to `Load`. Sets `m_KeyLoadStatus` to `Ok` on success or `WrongPassword` on failure; sets `NoKeysFile` if the path does not exist. This is the method the application should call when the user supplies a password interactively after startup.

- `GetCachedMasterPassword() → std::string` — Returns a plain `std::string` copy of the password held in `m_CachedMasterPassword`. Returns an empty string if no successful `Load`, `Save`, or `Unlock` has occurred. Intended for OAuth token-refresh callbacks that need to re-encrypt and persist updated credentials.

- `IsDirty() → bool` — Returns `true` if any CRUD mutation has been applied since the last successful load or save. Not mutex-protected; treat as advisory only in multithreaded contexts.

- `GetProvider(name) → ProviderConfig const*` — Shared-lock lookup by registry key. Returns a raw pointer into the `m_Providers` map, or `nullptr` if not found. **The pointer is valid only while the caller holds no lock that could be displaced by a write; do not cache it across write operations.**

- `GetDefaultProvider() → ProviderConfig const*` — Shared-lock lookup using `m_DefaultProviderName`. Returns `nullptr` if the name is empty or the named provider does not exist. Same pointer-lifetime caveat as `GetProvider`.

- `GetDefaultProviderName() → std::string` — Returns a copy of the current default provider key string under a shared lock.

- `GetProviderNames() → std::vector<std::string>` — Returns a snapshot of all registered provider key strings under a shared lock. Iteration order follows `std::unordered_map` — i.e. unspecified.

- `HasProviders() → bool` — Returns `true` if the registry is non-empty, under a shared lock.

- `AddProvider(name, config) → bool` — Acquires a unique (write) lock. Fails and logs a warning if `name` already exists. On success, moves `config` into the map, sets `m_Dirty = true`. Auto-promotes the new provider to default if it is the first entry.

- `UpdateProvider(name, config) → bool` — Acquires a unique lock. Fails if `name` is not found. Replaces the existing config in-place and sets `m_Dirty = true`. Does not change `m_DefaultProviderName`.

- `RemoveProvider(name) → bool` — Acquires a unique lock. Fails if `name` is not found. Erases the entry and sets `m_Dirty = true`. If the removed provider was the default, the new default is `m_Providers.begin()->first` (unordered, non-deterministic), or the name is cleared if the map is now empty.

- `SetDefaultProvider(name) → void` — Acquires a unique lock. Accepts an empty string (to clear the default). Rejects a non-empty name that is not in the registry and logs a warning; in that case the current default is unchanged.

- `GetKeyLoadStatus() / SetKeyLoadStatus(status)` — Unsynchronized accessors for the `KeyLoadStatus` enum. `SetKeyLoadStatus` is intended for the application bootstrap path to pre-set the status before `Unlock` is called.

- `SetKeysFilePath(path) / GetKeysFilePath()` — Store/retrieve the file path used by `Unlock`. Not mutex-protected; must be called before concurrent access begins.

---

**Collaborators:**

- `KeyEncryption` (`keys/keyEncryption.h`) — Provides `Encrypt(json, password) → vector<uint8_t>` and `Decrypt(blob, password) → string`. All encryption/decryption logic lives there; `KeyManager` treats the blob as opaque bytes.
- `SecureString` (`keys/secureString.h`) — Wraps the cached master password. Exposes `Set(string)` and `Get() → string_view`. The intent is that the password is held in memory with whatever additional protection `SecureString` provides (the implementation is external to this file).
- `simdjson` (`simdjson/simdjson.h`) — Used in `ParseProvidersJson` via the on-demand API (`ondemand::parser`, `padded_string`, `ondemand::document`, `ondemand::object`). Parsing is zero-copy/lazy over the padded string; the padded string must outlive the document iterator.
- `SecretRedactor` (`log/secretRedactor.h`) — `ParseProvidersJson` calls `SecretRedactor::Get().AddSecret(value)` for the `secret_access_key` and `session_token` fields of AWS providers immediately after parsing, so they are scrubbed from any subsequent log output.
- `engine.h` — Included for logging macros (`LOG_CORE_ERROR`, `LOG_CORE_WARN`, `LOG_CORE_INFO`).

---

**Threading / lifetime:**

`KeyManager` is designed for shared concurrent reads and exclusive writes. The internal `mutable std::shared_mutex m_Mutex` guards `m_Providers` and `m_DefaultProviderName`. All six read methods (`GetProvider`, `GetDefaultProvider`, `GetDefaultProviderName`, `GetProviderNames`, `HasProviders`) acquire a `std::shared_lock`; all four write methods (`AddProvider`, `UpdateProvider`, `RemoveProvider`, `SetDefaultProvider`) acquire a `std::unique_lock`.

`Load`, `Save`, `LoadPlaintext`, `SavePlaintext`, `LoadFromEnvironment`, and `ParseProvidersJson` acquire the mutex internally but are not safe to call concurrently with each other or with CRUD methods — they are intended to be called during initialization or in a single-threaded setup phase.

Raw pointers returned by `GetProvider` and `GetDefaultProvider` are interior pointers into the `unordered_map`. They are invalidated by any subsequent write operation (add, update, or remove) that triggers a rehash or erases the entry. Callers must not store these pointers beyond the scope of a single request that does not race with writers.

`m_KeyLoadStatus`, `m_KeysFilePath`, `m_CachedMasterPassword`, and `m_Dirty` are **not** protected by the mutex. They are safe to set during single-threaded startup before the engine begins processing concurrent requests.

`KeyManager` has no virtual functions, no internal threads, and no async callbacks. It is safe to destroy at any time after all concurrent callers have completed.

---

**Notes:**

- The JSON format is versioned (`"version": 1`). `ParseProvidersJson` hard-rejects any version other than 1, so future format changes require an explicit version bump and a migration path.
- `SerializeToJson` is a hand-rolled serializer using `ostringstream`. It applies JSON character escaping only to `m_Params` keys/values and PEM key data; all other fields (`m_DisplayName`, `m_Endpoint`, `m_ApiKey`, etc.) are written verbatim. If any of those fields contain `"`, `\`, or control characters, the output will be malformed JSON. Callers storing user-supplied strings in those fields should validate them before inserting.
- When `credential_type` is absent from a persisted JSON entry, `ParseProvidersJson` leaves `m_CredentialType` at its default-initialised value of `"api_key"`, ensuring backward compatibility with single-provider config files.
- `LoadFromEnvironment` calls `m_Providers.clear()` under a unique lock but does **not** reset `m_KeyLoadStatus`. If the caller bootstraps via this path, they must set the status manually.
- `RemoveProvider` selects a new default via `m_Providers.begin()`, which is non-deterministic for `unordered_map`. Applications that care about default-provider stability after removal should call `SetDefaultProvider` explicitly.
- `GetCachedMasterPassword` returns a `std::string` copy, which means the password briefly exists as a heap-allocated plain string. Code receiving this value should avoid logging it; `SecretRedactor` is not automatically notified of the returned copy.
- AWS `secret_access_key` and `session_token` are registered with `SecretRedactor` at parse time, but only for the `aws` credential type. Providers using `credential_type = "api_key"` with secrets in `m_Params` do not receive the same treatment automatically.

### 132_doc_engine_log_log_h

<a id="132-doc-engine-log-log-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/132_doc_engine_log_log_h/docu.output.md`



### LOW Potential Secrets Disclosure in Logs
- **Location:** Log class and initialization, throughout file (Log.{h,cpp}, ~lines 10-100)
- **Issue:** The provided log configuration, especially for SecurityLogger, writes all security-relevant information (including potentially sensitive events or error details) to disk (log/security.txt) and to the console/TUI. There are no safeguards in this layer to prevent secrets (token values, API keys, passwords) from being logged via security logger or app logger by accident.
- **Impact:** If higher-level application code logs sensitive information to these loggers, those secrets will be persisted to disk and/or visible to anyone reading the logs, leaking credentials or secrets. Attackers with access to logs could laterally escalate or move through the environment. 
- **Fix:** Enforce a clear logging policy at the call-site: never log secrets or sensitive material. Consider implementing log filtering/redaction or explicit static analysis checks for log statements that might inadvertently include secrets. Add documentation warnings for developers, and possibly runtime filters in the security logger sink(s) to redact known sensitive types.

---

### NONE
No other security issues identified.


---

### 133_doc_engine_keys_oauthTokenManager_h

<a id="133-doc-engine-keys-oauthtokenmanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/133_doc_engine_keys_oauthTokenManager_h/docu.output.md`



## OAuthTokenManager

**Role:** `OAuthTokenManager` owns the complete OAuth 2.0 token lifecycle for every named provider credential registered in j9t's `KeyManager`. It maintains an in-memory map of `TokenEntry` records — each holding an access token, refresh token, token endpoint URL, client ID, client secret, and Unix-second expiry timestamp — and runs a single background thread that wakes every 30 seconds to proactively refresh any token within 5 minutes of expiry. On first use after startup it can also perform a synchronous on-demand refresh from within `GetAccessToken` for entries that were hydrated from persisted storage with no live access token. All token strings are registered with and rotated through `SecretRedactor` so they are never emitted in plain text to logs.

---

**Public API:**

- `OAuthTokenManager(KeyManager& keyManager) → void` — Constructor. Stores a non-owning reference to `KeyManager`. Does not start the background thread; callers must call `Start()` explicitly.

- `~OAuthTokenManager()` — Destructor. Calls `Stop()` unconditionally, joining the background thread before the object is destroyed.

- `Start() → void` — Calls `HydrateFromKeyManager()`, sets `m_Running = true`, and spawns the background `RefreshLoop` thread. Idempotent: a second call while already running returns immediately. Logs at INFO level.

- `Stop() → void` — Sets `m_Running = false`, notifies `m_Cv` to wake the sleeping refresh thread, and blocks until the thread joins. Idempotent if the thread is not running. Logs at INFO level.

- `HydrateFromKeyManager() → void` — Iterates all provider names from `KeyManager::GetProviderNames()`, skipping any provider whose `m_CredentialType` is not `"oauth"` or whose `m_RefreshToken` is empty. For eligible providers that do not already have an entry in `m_Tokens`, creates a `TokenEntry` with an empty access token and `m_ExpiresAt = 0`, which forces an on-demand refresh on the first `GetAccessToken` call. Registers the refresh token (and client secret, if present) with `SecretRedactor`. Also called automatically by `Start()`. Safe to call before `Start()`.

- `GetAccessToken(keyName, errorMessage) → std::string` — Returns a valid access token for the named credential. If the stored entry has an empty access token, `m_ExpiresAt == 0`, or is already past its expiry, it performs a **synchronous** HTTP refresh before returning. If another thread is concurrently refreshing the same entry, the call blocks on `m_Cv` until the refresh completes. Returns an empty string and writes a human-readable message into `errorMessage` on any failure (credential not found, refresh HTTP error, JSON parse error, missing `access_token` field). Does not throw.

- `StoreTokens(keyName, accessToken, refreshToken, expiresInSeconds, tokenEndpoint, clientId, clientSecret) → void` — Upserts a `TokenEntry` for `keyName` with the supplied tokens, computing `m_ExpiresAt` as `now + expiresInSeconds`. Registers all non-empty secret strings with `SecretRedactor`. Intended to be called at the end of an interactive OAuth consent flow (e.g., after exchanging an authorization code). The `tokenEndpoint`, `clientId`, and `clientSecret` parameters are optional; omitting them disables background refresh for this entry.

- `HasValidToken(keyName) → bool` — Returns `true` if an entry exists for `keyName` and either `m_ExpiresAt == 0` (indeterminate — see Notes) or the current time is strictly less than `m_ExpiresAt`. Does **not** trigger a refresh; purely observational. Thread-safe via `m_Mutex`.

---

**Collaborators:**

- `KeyManager` — Provides the persisted OAuth configuration (`m_CredentialType`, `m_RefreshToken`, `m_TokenEndpoint`, `m_ClientId`, `m_ClientSecret`) via `GetProviderNames()` and `GetProvider(name)`. `OAuthTokenManager` holds a non-owning reference; `KeyManager` must outlive it.
- `SecretRedactor` — Singleton accessed via `SecretRedactor::Get()`. Every access token, refresh token, and client secret is added on creation and removed (`RemoveSecret`) before being replaced during token rotation, preventing stale tokens from lingering in the redactor set.
- `CurlWrapper` — Supplies the CA bundle path via `CurlWrapper::GetCaBundlePath()`, which is applied to every `curl_easy_setopt(CURLOPT_CAINFO, …)` call in `RefreshToken`.
- `libcurl` (raw `curl_easy_*`) — `RefreshToken` instantiates raw CURL handles directly (not via `CurlWrapper`'s higher-level helpers) with a 30-second timeout and `application/x-www-form-urlencoded` content type.
- `simdjson` (`ondemand` API) — Parses the token endpoint JSON response in `RefreshToken` to extract `access_token`, `refresh_token`, and `expires_in`.
- `engine.h` / logging macros (`LOG_CORE_INFO`, `LOG_CORE_WARN`, `LOG_CORE_ERROR`) — Used throughout for operational logging.

---

**Threading / lifetime:**

- **Ownership:** The caller (typically the engine's startup path) owns the `OAuthTokenManager` instance and must ensure the referenced `KeyManager` outlives it.
- **Background thread:** `Start()` spawns `m_RefreshThread`. The thread is joined by `Stop()`, which is called by the destructor, so destruction is always safe as long as it happens on a thread that is not the refresh thread itself.
- **Locking:** `m_Mutex` guards `m_Tokens` for all reads and writes. `GetAccessToken` uses `std::unique_lock` and waits on `m_Cv` for in-flight refreshes. `RefreshToken` is called with the lock released (the caller sets `m_Refreshing = true`, drops the lock, calls `RefreshToken`, re-acquires, clears `m_Refreshing`, and notifies). `HasValidToken` uses `std::lock_guard`. `m_Running` is `std::atomic<bool>` and accessed without holding `m_Mutex`.
- **Re-entrancy:** `RefreshToken` directly mutates the `TokenEntry` passed by reference while the lock is not held; callers must ensure no other thread can destroy the entry concurrently. The `m_Refreshing` flag and `m_Cv` wait in `GetAccessToken` prevent two threads from refreshing the same entry simultaneously.

---

**Notes:**

- `m_ExpiresAt == 0` is a dual-meaning sentinel: it indicates either a freshly hydrated (from-disk) entry whose access token has not yet been obtained, **or** an entry stored via `StoreTokens` with `expiresInSeconds == 0`. In `HasValidToken` this condition returns `true` (treated as valid), but in `GetAccessToken` it triggers an immediate refresh. Callers should not interpret `HasValidToken() == true` as proof that `GetAccessToken` will not perform network I/O.
- `HydrateFromKeyManager` skips providers already present in `m_Tokens` (guarded by a `find` check inside the lock). This means an in-session `StoreTokens` call for a provider takes precedence over the persisted version and will not be overwritten by a later `HydrateFromKeyManager` call.
- Token rotation is handled silently: if the refresh response includes a new `refresh_token` field, `RefreshToken` replaces `entry.m_RefreshToken` and updates `SecretRedactor` accordingly. The rotated token is **not** persisted back to `KeyManager`; restarts will re-read the original persisted refresh token.
- `RefreshToken` defaults `expires_in` to 3600 seconds if the field is absent or unparsable in the response JSON.
- The background `RefreshLoop` iterates `m_Tokens` while holding `m_Mutex`, drops the lock to call `RefreshToken` for one entry, then re-acquires. This means the loop processes at most one expiring token per 30-second sleep cycle per map iteration pass; if multiple tokens are near expiry they will each be handled in the same iteration of the `for` loop but one at a time.
- `RefreshToken` constructs the POST body by string concatenation without URL-encoding the token values. Refresh tokens containing `&` or `=` characters would silently produce a malformed request body.

### 134_doc_engine_keys_secureString_h

<a id="134-doc-engine-keys-securestring-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/134_doc_engine_keys_secureString_h/docu.output.md`



## SecureString (`keys/secureString.h` / `keys/secureString.cpp`)

**Role:** `SecureString` is a RAII container for sensitive byte strings — master passwords, API keys, and similar secrets — within the `AIAssistant` namespace. It allocates heap memory via `std::malloc`, pins it in physical RAM with `mlock()`/`VirtualLock()` to prevent the OS from writing it to swap, and guarantees that the contents are overwritten with zeroes (using `explicit_bzero`, `SecureZeroMemory`, `memset_s`, or a volatile-pointer loop as appropriate for the platform) before the memory is ever freed or reused. It is not a general-purpose string: copy construction and copy assignment are explicitly deleted so secrets cannot be silently duplicated.

**Public API:**

- `Set(std::string_view value) → void` — Copies the bytes of `value` into the locked buffer. If the existing allocation is large enough (`m_Capacity >= value.size() + 1`) the buffer is reused in place, deliberately avoiding a fresh allocation that could leave the previous secret elsewhere in the heap. If reallocation is needed, `Release()` is called first (zeroing + unlocking the old buffer) before `std::malloc` and `LockMemory`. Stores a trailing NUL so the buffer is a valid C string. Logs `LOG_CORE_ERROR` and leaves the object empty if allocation fails; logs `LOG_CORE_WARN` if `mlock`/`VirtualLock` fails (object is still usable, swap protection is simply absent).

- `Get() const → std::string_view` — Returns a non-owning view into the locked buffer. Returns an empty view when `m_Size == 0` or `m_Buffer` is null. **The view is invalidated by any subsequent call to `Set()`, `Clear()`, `Release()`, or destruction** — callers must not store it across those operations.

- `Clear() → void` — Zeroes the entire allocated capacity via `SecureZero` and resets `m_Size` to 0, but **does not free or unlock the buffer**. The allocation is retained for potential reuse by the next `Set()` call.

- `IsEmpty() const → bool` — Returns `true` when `m_Size == 0`.

- `Size() const → size_t` — Returns the current content length in bytes, not including the trailing NUL.

- Move constructor / move-assignment operator — Transfer buffer ownership without any copy or additional allocation; the moved-from object is left in a valid empty state (`m_Buffer = nullptr`, sizes zeroed). Self-assignment is guarded in the assignment operator.

**Collaborators:**

- `engine.h` — Included for the `LOG_CORE_ERROR` and `LOG_CORE_WARN` macros used when allocation or memory locking fails. No other engine functionality is consumed.
- Platform memory APIs — `mlock`/`munlock` (POSIX, `<sys/mman.h>`) and `VirtualLock`/`VirtualUnlock` + `SecureZeroMemory` (Windows, `<windows.h>`) are used directly; no abstraction layer sits between `SecureString` and the OS.
- Callers in the key/secrets subsystem (e.g. master-password holders, API-key stores) are expected to be the sole owners; nothing in the codebase should hold a raw pointer or `string_view` into a `SecureString` that it does not also own.

**Threading / lifetime:**

- `SecureString` has no internal synchronisation. Concurrent calls to any mutating method (`Set`, `Clear`, move-assignment, destruction) against the same instance require external locking by the caller.
- `Get()` is safe to call concurrently with other `Get()` calls on the same instance only if no mutation is occurring simultaneously.
- Ownership is exclusive (copy disabled, move-only). Whoever constructs or receives a moved `SecureString` is responsible for its lifetime. Do not pass raw `m_Buffer` pointers or `Get()` views to threads that might outlive the owning `SecureString`.
- Destruction calls `Release()`, which zeroes and unlocks before `std::free` — there is no deferred or async cleanup.

**Notes:**

- `mlock` failure is non-fatal by design: the object logs a warning and continues with an unlockable buffer. Callers operating in high-security contexts (key derivation, credential storage) should check whether locking actually succeeded; as coded there is no public accessor for that status, so callers who need to enforce it must verify `mlock` limits at startup independently.
- `Clear()` retains the allocation. If you `Clear()` a `SecureString` and never `Set()` it again, the locked page remains pinned until `Release()` is called (i.e. destruction or move-assignment). This matters in environments with tight `RLIMIT_MEMLOCK` budgets.
- The buffer always carries a trailing NUL (`m_Buffer[value.size()] = '\0'`), making `m_Buffer` safe to pass to C APIs expecting a null-terminated string, but `Get()` returns a `string_view` of length `m_Size` excluding that NUL.
- The volatile-pointer zeroing fallback handles Apple platforms where `memset_s` requires `__STDC_WANT_LIB_EXT1__` to be defined before `<string.h>` and `explicit_bzero` is not guaranteed; it is the last resort and relies on the compiler not optimising through a `volatile unsigned char*`.
- A process with `ptrace` privileges, `/proc/<pid>/mem` access, or kernel-level access can still read the locked pages. The protection model targets swap-file forensics and heap-reuse leakage only — not live memory inspection.

### 135_doc_engine_log_log_h

<a id="135-doc-engine-log-log-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/135_doc_engine_log_log_h/docu.output.md`



## AIAssistant::Log

**Role:** `Log` is the centralised logging facility for the j9t / AIAssistant engine. Its constructor creates and registers three named spdlog loggers — `Engine`, `Application`, and `Security` — all of which share a single thread-safe `ostream_sink_mt` that writes to `std::cout`. The `Security` logger additionally writes to a rotating file (`log/security.txt`, 10 MB per file, 5 file rotation). The constructor also ensures the `log/` directory exists. All three loggers are set to `trace` level with flush-on-every-message. Accessor methods expose raw references to the underlying `spdlog::logger` objects for use by the rest of the engine.

**Public API:**

- `Log()` — Constructor. Creates all three loggers and registers them with spdlog's global registry via `spdlog::register_logger`. As a side effect it calls `std::filesystem::create_directories("log")` to guarantee the log output directory exists before the rotating file sink is constructed. No arguments. Throws if spdlog sink or logger construction fails.

- `GetLogger() → spdlog::logger&` — Returns a reference to the `Engine` logger (`m_Logger`). Intended for low-level engine/infrastructure log calls. The lifetime of the returned reference is bound to the `Log` instance.

- `GetAppLogger() → spdlog::logger&` — Returns a reference to the `Application` logger (`m_AppLogger`). Intended for application-layer (workflow, agent, integration) log calls. Same lifetime constraint as above.

- `GetSecurityLogger() → spdlog::logger&` — Returns a reference to the `Security` logger (`m_SecurityLogger`). This logger emits to both the console sink and the rotating file. Use it for all authentication, secrets, webhook validation, and signing events. Same lifetime constraint as above.

**Collaborators:**

- **spdlog** (`spdlog/spdlog.h`, `ostream_sink.h`, `basic_file_sink.h`, `rotating_file_sink.h`) — the sole external dependency. All sink and logger objects are spdlog types.
- **`JarvisAgent::OnStart()`** — documented inline in the constructor: after `Log` is constructed, `JarvisAgent::OnStart()` redirects `std::cout.rdbuf()` to a `TerminalLogStreamBuf` (an ncurses-backed stream buffer). Because the ostream sink holds a reference to `std::cout`, this redirection silently routes all three loggers' console output through the TUI without any further change to `Log` itself. Callers must be aware that the effective destination of console log output changes after `JarvisAgent::OnStart()` runs.
- **`std::filesystem`** — used directly to create the `log/` directory.

**Threading / lifetime:**

- Both sinks are instantiated with the `_mt` (multi-thread) variants (`ostream_sink_mt`, `rotating_file_sink_mt`), so concurrent calls to any of the three loggers are safe.
- The `Log` object owns all three `shared_ptr<spdlog::logger>` members. The loggers are *also* registered in spdlog's global registry, so they remain alive at least as long as the `Log` instance; destroying the `Log` object does not automatically deregister the loggers from the global registry.
- References returned by `GetLogger()`, `GetAppLogger()`, and `GetSecurityLogger()` are valid only while the owning `Log` instance is alive. Callers must not cache these references beyond the lifetime of the `Log` object.
- The `ostreamSink` `shared_ptr` is a local variable in the constructor but is captured inside both the general sink vector and the security sink vector, so its reference count remains ≥ 1 for the lifetime of any logger that uses it.

**Notes:**

- All log patterns deliberately omit ANSI colour codes. The inline comment explicitly states this is because ncurses cannot handle them; adding colour sequences to the pattern will break TUI rendering after `JarvisAgent::OnStart()` redirects `std::cout`.
- The `Security` logger applies a *separate* pattern directly on its file sink (`[Security]` channel name hardcoded in the pattern string), distinct from the shared ostream sink pattern. Output format between the two sinks for security events is therefore not identical.
- `flush_on(spdlog::level::trace)` is set on all three loggers, meaning every single log call triggers a flush. This is intentional for reliability but has a measurable performance cost under high-frequency logging; avoid using the `Engine` or `Application` loggers in tight inner loops.
- The rotating file path `log/security.txt` is relative to the process working directory, not an absolute path. If the working directory changes after construction, the already-open file handle is unaffected, but the `create_directories` call at construction time must have succeeded against the same working directory.
- Because the loggers are registered globally with spdlog, a second `Log` instance constructed in the same process will attempt to register loggers with names already present (`Engine`, `Application`, `Security`) and spdlog will throw `spdlog::spdlog_ex`. Only one `Log` instance should exist per process.

### 136_doc_engine_log_secretRedactor_h

<a id="136-doc-engine-log-secretredactor-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/136_doc_engine_log_secretRedactor_h/docu.output.md`



## SecretRedactor

**Role:** `SecretRedactor` is a process-wide singleton that sits in the logging pipeline and scrubs sensitive values — API keys, tokens, passwords, or any other registered secret — from log messages before they are written. Every log call that may contain externally-sourced credentials should pass its message through `Redact()`. The class lives in `log/secretRedactor.h/.cpp` inside the `AIAssistant` namespace and is the single authoritative registry of secrets that must never appear in output.

**Public API:**

- `Get() → SecretRedactor&` — returns the Meyer's-singleton instance. Safe to call from any thread at any point in the program lifetime; the static local is initialised once.

- `AddSecret(const std::string& secret)` — registers a secret value for redaction. Silently ignores strings shorter than 4 characters to avoid spurious matches on common short tokens. Performs a linear duplicate check before inserting, so registering the same value twice is safe and idempotent. Acquires `m_Mutex` internally.

- `RemoveSecret(const std::string& secret)` — deregisters a previously registered secret; intended for use after token rotation so the old credential is no longer held in memory. Uses erase-remove idiom on `m_Secrets`. Acquires `m_Mutex` internally. No-op if the value was never registered.

- `Redact(const std::string& message) → std::string` — returns a copy of `message` with every occurrence of every registered secret replaced by the literal string `[REDACTED]`. Replacement is performed sequentially per secret using `std::string::find` in a forward-walking loop, so overlapping secrets are each handled independently. Returns the original message unchanged (no copy) if the internal secrets vector is empty. Acquires `m_Mutex` internally.

- `HasSecrets() → bool` — returns `true` if at least one secret is currently registered. Useful as a fast guard before calling `Redact()` in a hot logging path, though `Redact()` already does the same check internally. Acquires `m_Mutex` internally.

**Collaborators:** The class is self-contained and has no outbound dependencies beyond the C++ standard library (`<mutex>`, `<string>`, `<vector>`, `<algorithm>`). It is consumed by whatever logging pipeline exists in j9t — callers such as log formatters or log sink implementations are expected to call `Redact()` on message strings. Secret values are expected to be fed in by the secrets-management layer (e.g., at credential load time or after a webhook-triggered token rotation) and removed when those credentials are retired.

**Threading / lifetime:** The singleton is created on first call to `Get()` and lives for the duration of the process. `m_Mutex` is declared `mutable` so `Redact()` and `HasSecrets()` can lock from a `const` context. All four public methods acquire the same `std::mutex` via `std::lock_guard`, making the entire public API thread-safe. There is no re-entrant locking — callers must not call any `SecretRedactor` method while already holding `m_Mutex` from another code path, though in practice external callers have no access to that mutex.

**Notes:**

- The minimum length threshold is exactly 4 characters (`MIN_SECRET_LENGTH = 4`). A secret of length 1–3 will be silently discarded by `AddSecret()` with no error or warning; callers should validate credentials before registering them if they need to detect this case.
- Replacement advances `pos` by `REDACTED_LEN` (10, the length of `[REDACTED]`) after each substitution, not by the original secret length. This means a message containing two overlapping occurrences of a secret that straddles a replacement boundary will still be processed correctly without risking infinite loops.
- Secrets are stored and matched as exact byte strings; there is no case-folding, Unicode normalisation, or regex support. A credential that appears in a different case or encoding than the registered form will not be redacted.
- Duplicate prevention in `AddSecret()` uses a linear scan over `m_Secrets`. If a large number of distinct secrets are registered this becomes O(n) per insertion, but the expected secret count in practice is small (single-digit to low tens).
- `RemoveSecret()` removes all matching entries if somehow duplicates exist, because `std::remove` erases every element equal to the target value before the `erase` call.
- The `[REDACTED]` replacement string and the minimum length are internal `constexpr` values with no public accessor; they cannot be configured at runtime.
