### 137_doc_engine_log_terminalLogStreamBuf_h

<a id="137-doc-engine-log-terminallogstreambuf-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/137_doc_engine_log_terminalLogStreamBuf_h/docu.output.md`



## TerminalLogStreamBuf

**Role:** `TerminalLogStreamBuf` is a custom `std::streambuf` implementation that acts as a fan-out sink for log output within the `AIAssistant` workflow engine. When installed as the buffer of a `std::ostream` (e.g. `std::cout` or a dedicated log stream), it intercepts character output, accumulates it line-by-line, strips ANSI escape sequences, and simultaneously dispatches each completed line to three destinations: the `TerminalManager` UI queue, a persistent log file, and an optional broadcast callback. Its purpose is to unify all log traffic — regardless of source — into a consistent, clean, multi-target pipeline without requiring call sites to know about any individual sink.

**Public API:**

- `TerminalLogStreamBuf(TerminalManager* terminalManager, std::shared_ptr<std::ofstream> fileLogger)` — Constructs the stream buffer. `terminalManager` may be `nullptr` (the terminal sink is skipped silently). `fileLogger` is a shared file stream; if it is null or not open, file output is skipped. Ownership of the `ofstream` is shared via `std::shared_ptr`, so multiple objects may reference the same file handle.

- `SetLogBroadcastCallback(std::function<void(std::string const&)> callback) → void` — Registers (or replaces) a callback that receives every flushed, ANSI-stripped log line. The callback is invoked synchronously from whichever thread triggers a flush. Passing an empty `std::function` disables the callback. This method is thread-safe; it acquires `m_CallbackMutex` internally.

**Collaborators:**

- `TerminalManager` (`log/terminalManager.h`) — Receives completed log lines via `EnqueueLogLine(std::string)`. The pointer is non-owning and is expected to outlive this object. If `TerminalManager` is null the terminal sink is bypassed without error.
- `std::ofstream` (shared) — Holds the open log file. The `std::shared_ptr` allows the file handle to be shared with other subsystems (e.g. a rotating log manager). `TerminalLogStreamBuf` locks `m_FileMutex` before each write and calls `flush()` immediately after, so every line is durably written.
- Any `std::ostream` this buffer is installed into — callers redirect an `ostream`'s `rdbuf()` to point at this object; the stream itself is not owned here.

**Threading / lifetime:**

Three separate mutexes guard distinct resources:

- `m_BufferMutex` — protects `m_Buffer` and all `streambuf` virtual overrides (`sync`, `overflow`, `xsputn`). All three overrides acquire this lock before touching the buffer or calling `syncLocked()`. `syncLocked()` must only be called while `m_BufferMutex` is already held.
- `m_FileMutex` — protects writes to `*m_FileLogger` inside `syncLocked()`, allowing the file handle to be safely shared across threads.
- `m_CallbackMutex` — protects `m_LogBroadcastCallback` for both reads in `syncLocked()` and writes in `SetLogBroadcastCallback()`.

The `TerminalManager` raw pointer must remain valid for the entire lifetime of this object; there is no lifetime tracking. The `std::shared_ptr<std::ofstream>` keeps the file alive as long as any co-owner holds a reference. This class is not copyable or movable (mutexes are non-copyable); it should be heap-allocated or held by reference.

**Notes:**

- Line buffering is the invariant: `syncLocked()` is triggered only on `'\n'` or an explicit `sync()` call. Partial lines accumulate in `m_Buffer` indefinitely until a newline arrives or `sync()` is called externally (e.g. when the stream is flushed or destroyed).
- `StripAnsi()` removes ANSI CSI/SGR escape sequences before writing to all sinks. The stripping logic ends an escape sequence on any ASCII letter (`A–Z`, `a–z`), which covers SGR (`m`) and most cursor-control codes, but does not handle sequences terminated by non-letter final bytes (e.g. some private-use sequences). The original ANSI text is never forwarded anywhere; only the cleaned string is distributed.
- An empty line after stripping (e.g. a line that was purely an ANSI sequence) is silently discarded — none of the three sinks receive it.
- `syncLocked()` always returns `0` (success). It never signals a write error back through the `streambuf` protocol, even if the file write or callback throws.
- The callback is invoked while `m_BufferMutex` is still held (since `syncLocked()` runs inside the lock). Callbacks that themselves write to the same stream will deadlock.


---

### 138_doc_engine_log_terminalManager_h

<a id="138-doc-engine-log-terminalmanager-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/138_doc_engine_log_terminalManager_h/docu.output.md`



## TerminalManager

**Role:** `TerminalManager` owns the full-screen terminal UI for the j9t/AIAssistant engine, implemented with PDCursesMod. It divides the terminal into four PDCurses windows: a `[ LOG ]` header, a scrolling log content area, a `[ STATUS ]` header, and a status content area whose height is controlled by caller-supplied callbacks. It handles initialization and teardown of the curses session (including locale, cursor visibility, `termios` save/restore on POSIX), detects terminal resize on every render cycle, drains a thread-safe queue of log lines into the log window, and calls out to registered callbacks to populate the status panel. It also sanitizes all text through `SanitizeForCurses` before passing it to PDCursesMod to prevent assertion failures caused by out-of-range Unicode codepoints or malformed UTF-8 bytes in AI response content.

---

**Public API:**

- `TerminalManager()` — constructs the pImpl (`Impl`) via `make_unique`. No curses state is touched.

- `~TerminalManager()` — calls `Shutdown()`. Safe to destruct without a prior explicit `Shutdown()`.

- `Initialize() → void` — enters the curses session: calls `setlocale`, saves `termios` (POSIX only), runs `initscr`, disables the cursor, enables `cbreak`/`noecho`/`nodelay`, applies the color theme (attempts `init_color` to set `COLOR_GREEN` to the dashboard green `#02a414` in 0–1000 units if `can_change_color()`), then calls `RecreateWindowsIfNeeded` to lay out all four windows. On POSIX, skips initialization entirely if `STDOUT_FILENO` is not a TTY, leaving `m_Initialized = false`. Idempotent — a second call is a no-op.

- `Shutdown() → void` — tears down the four PDCurses windows with `delwin`, calls `endwin`, and on POSIX restores the saved `termios` via `tcsetattr(STDIN_FILENO, TCSANOW, ...)`. Diagnostic progress messages are written to stderr using raw POSIX `write` / Windows `_write` in debug builds (`NDEBUG` not defined) to avoid any dependency on spdlog or this object's own logging path during shutdown. Idempotent — a second call is a no-op.

- `Render() → void` — the normal per-frame call. Calls `HandleResize` (recreates windows if terminal dimensions changed), `DrainQueuedLogLines` (moves pending lines from the mutex-protected queue into the log window, scrolling as needed), then `RenderStatus` (erases the status window, invokes `m_StatusLinesCallback` to obtain lines, writes them sanitized, and refreshes all four windows). Does nothing if not initialized.

- `RenderPaused(int counter) → void` — alternative render path for a paused engine state. Handles resize, then overwrites the log window with a `*** PAUSED (press 'p' to resume) ***` banner and the supplied `counter` value, and then renders the status panel normally. Does nothing if not initialized.

- `EnqueueLogLine(std::string const& line) → void` — the only method safe to call from any thread. Acquires `m_LogMutex` and appends `line` to `m_PendingLines`. The line is not sanitized or rendered here; that happens on the next `Render()` call on the render thread.

- `SetStatusCallbacks(StatusLinesCallback, StatusHeightCallback) → void` — stores both callbacks (moved into `Impl`). `StatusHeightCallback` has signature `int(int totalRows)` and returns the desired number of status content rows; `StatusLinesCallback` has signature `void(std::vector<std::string>& lines, int maxWidth)` and is expected to populate `lines` in place. If already initialized, immediately calls `RecreateWindowsIfNeeded` so the layout adjusts to the new height preference. Either or both callbacks may be null; the code guards all call sites.

---

**Collaborators:**

- **PDCursesMod** (`pdcursesmod/curses.h`) — the sole rendering backend. All window creation, attribute setting, printing, and refresh go through its API (`newwin`, `delwin`, `wattron`, `mvwprintw`, `wscrl`, `wclrtoeol`, `wrefresh`, `scrollok`, `idlok`, etc.).
- **`engine.h`** — included in the `.cpp`; the engine drives the render loop and supplies the status callbacks, but `TerminalManager` holds no direct pointer to `Engine`.
- **Caller-supplied callbacks** — `StatusLinesCallback` and `StatusHeightCallback` are the primary integration point for the rest of the system to project runtime state into the status panel.
- **`SanitizeForCurses` (file-local)** — every string passed to PDCursesMod goes through this function to replace C0 control characters (except TAB), DEL (`0x7F`), malformed UTF-8 sequences, and codepoints above `U+10FFFF` with `'?'` or `' '`.

---

**Threading / lifetime:**

- `TerminalManager` is expected to be owned by a single controlling object (the engine). Lifetime is straightforward RAII via `unique_ptr<Impl>`.
- **`EnqueueLogLine` is the only thread-safe method.** It is guarded by `m_LogMutex`. All other methods — `Initialize`, `Shutdown`, `Render`, `RenderPaused`, `SetStatusCallbacks` — must be called from the same thread (the render/main thread). Calling them from multiple threads simultaneously is not safe.
- `DrainQueuedLogLines` acquires `m_LogMutex` briefly to swap `m_PendingLines` into a local vector, then releases the lock before any PDCurses calls, keeping the critical section minimal.
- The `StatusLinesCallback` and `StatusHeightCallback` are invoked on the render thread inside `Render()` and `RecreateWindowsIfNeeded()`. Callers must ensure those callbacks are safe to call from the render thread.

---

**Notes:**

- **Window recreation resets `m_LogPrintLine` to 0**, discarding the concept of where the next log line should go. Any log lines already rendered in the old window are visually lost on resize; the log window starts blank after a resize event.
- **`wscrl` / scroll behavior**: once `m_LogPrintLine` reaches the log window's row count, `wscrl(m_LogWindow, 1)` is called to scroll up one line and the last row is cleared before writing. This relies on `scrollok(TRUE)` and `idlok(TRUE)` being set on the log window.
- **`wclrtoeol` before every `mvwprintw`** is deliberate in both the log and status paths to avoid orphaned `MAX_UNICODE` continuation cells left by wide characters from a prior render — a known PDCursesMod assertion failure (`PDC_transform_line`) that manifests under high log churn or AI output containing emoji.
- **`termios` safety net on POSIX**: PDCursesMod's VT backend may not fully restore terminal attributes on `endwin`. `Shutdown` unconditionally calls `tcsetattr(STDIN_FILENO, TCSANOW, ...)` with the pre-`initscr` snapshot to prevent the terminal being left in `noecho`/`cbreak` state after process exit.
- **Debug-only raw stderr in `Shutdown`**: the `RAW_STDERR` macro is compiled out when `NDEBUG` is defined. In debug builds it emits bracketed progress markers (`[TM] delwin LogWindow`, etc.) directly via `write`/`_write` to avoid re-entering any logging infrastructure that might itself reference `TerminalManager`.
- **No-TTY guard**: on non-Windows platforms, `Initialize` checks `isatty(STDOUT_FILENO)` and silently returns without setting `m_Initialized` if stdout is not a terminal (e.g., redirected pipeline). All subsequent `Render*` calls then short-circuit cleanly.
- **Color index reuse**: `COLOR_GREEN` (index 2) is mutated globally via `init_color` when `can_change_color()` is true. This affects any other curses application sharing the same terminal session that also uses `COLOR_GREEN`, which is not a concern in practice since the engine owns the terminal exclusively while initialized.


---

### 139_doc_engine_auxiliary_TracyClient_cpp

<a id="139-doc-engine-auxiliary-tracyclient-cpp"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/139_doc_engine_auxiliary_TracyClient_cpp/docu.output.md`



## TracyClient.cpp

**Role:** This is the single-file amalgamation entry point for the Tracy profiler client library. Its sole job is to `#include` every Tracy client-side translation unit in one place so that the embedding project can add profiling support by compiling exactly one `.cpp` file. When `TRACY_ENABLE` is not defined the file reduces to including `TracySystem.cpp` only, giving a zero-overhead build with no profiler symbols. When `TRACY_ENABLE` is defined it pulls in the full client stack: LZ4 compression, the profiler core, callstack unwinding, power/CPU-time/kernel-trace sampling, socket transport, the rpmalloc allocator, DXT1 texture encoding, the allocation tracker, operator `new`/`delete` overrides, KCore Linux tracing, and — conditionally — the libbacktrace DWARF/ELF/Mach-O symbol resolution back-end.

**Public API:**

This file exposes no functions or classes of its own. All public Tracy API surface (`TRACY_ZONE`, `ZoneScoped`, `TracyAlloc`, etc.) is declared in the Tracy headers and implemented by the translation units included here. Compiling this file satisfies the linker requirements for all of those headers.

**Collaborators:**

- `common/TracySystem.cpp` — always included; provides platform thread-naming utilities used by the rest of the client.
- `common/tracy_lz4.cpp` — LZ4 frame compression for the wire protocol.
- `client/TracyProfiler.cpp` — the profiler singleton, ring buffer, and network serialisation loop; the central dependency of the entire client.
- `client/TracyCallstack.cpp` — callstack capture entry points; delegates to platform unwinders.
- `client/TracySysPower.cpp`, `TracySysTime.cpp`, `TracySysTrace.cpp` — optional OS-level sampling (power counters, CPU time, kernel context-switch events).
- `common/TracySocket.cpp` — TCP socket wrapper used by `TracyProfiler.cpp` to stream data to the Tracy server.
- `client/tracy_rpmalloc.cpp` — embedded lock-free allocator used internally so that Tracy's own heap traffic does not recursively trigger its allocation hooks.
- `client/TracyDxt1.cpp` — GPU screenshot compression.
- `client/TracyAlloc.cpp` — allocation event helpers.
- `client/TracyOverride.cpp` — global `operator new` / `operator delete` overrides for automatic heap tracking.
- `client/TracyKCore.cpp` — Linux KCore/perf integration.
- `libbacktrace/` (`alloc`, `dwarf`, `fileline`, `mmapio`, `posix`, `sort`, `state`, plus `elf` or `macho`) — compiled when `TRACY_HAS_CALLSTACK` is 2, 3, 4, or 6; provides DWARF symbol resolution on Linux/macOS without depending on libunwind or addr2line.
- `common/TracyStackFrames.cpp` — symbol name table, compiled alongside libbacktrace.
- On MSVC, `ws2_32.lib`, `dbghelp.lib`, `advapi32.lib`, and `user32.lib` are auto-linked via `#pragma comment(lib, ...)`.

**Threading / lifetime:**

All lifetime and threading rules belong to `TracyProfiler.cpp`, not to this file. The profiler is a process-wide singleton initialised on first use via a static constructor inside `TracyProfiler.cpp`. It spawns its own background network thread. Callers must not attempt to explicitly construct or destroy the profiler object; it manages its own lifetime. `TracyOverride.cpp` replaces global allocation operators for the duration of the process, so any code in the same binary that calls `new`/`delete` after the profiler is initialised will have those calls intercepted and queued.

**Notes:**

- The file must be compiled as a single translation unit — do not compile the individual `client/` or `common/` `.cpp` files separately alongside it; doing so causes duplicate symbol errors.
- Removing `TRACY_ENABLE` from the build entirely is the correct way to ship a non-profiling build; do not just unlink the file, because the headers emit inline stubs only when the macro is absent at the point each header is included.
- MSVC warning level is forced to 0 (`#pragma warning(push, 0)`) around all Tracy sources to suppress noise from third-party code; this affects only the Tracy translation units, not surrounding project code.
- `TRACY_HAS_CALLSTACK` values 2, 3, 4, and 6 correspond to specific platform/unwinder combinations (ELF Linux, ELF with frame pointers, Mach-O macOS, and others as defined in Tracy's platform headers). Value 4 exclusively uses the Mach-O back-end; all others use ELF.
- On multi-DLL projects Tracy's own documentation recommends compiling this file into the main executable or a single designated DLL only, because the profiler singleton and the allocator overrides must exist exactly once per process.


---

### 13_doc_application_cloud_cloudConnectionPool_h

<a id="13-doc-application-cloud-cloudconnectionpool-h"></a>

*Source doc:* `../../../queue/jarvisCppDocu/13_doc_application_cloud_cloudConnectionPool_h/docu.output.md`



## CloudConnectionPool

**Role:** `CloudConnectionPool` is a generic, thread-safe object pool for persistent, non-HTTP connections in the j9t workflow engine — specifically targeting protocols such as PostgreSQL via libpq and future IMAP keep-alive connections. It manages a per-name pool of opaque `void*` connection handles, enforcing configurable limits on pool depth, idle lifetime, and acquire wait timeouts. HTTP-based integrations are explicitly excluded because libcurl handles their connection reuse internally via `CURLOPT_TCP_KEEPALIVE`; this pool exists solely for protocol stacks that lack built-in connection reuse.

---

**Public API:**

- `CloudConnectionPool()` — Default-constructs with default `Config` values: 4 connections per name, 300-second idle eviction, 10-second acquire timeout.

- `explicit CloudConnectionPool(Config config)` — Constructs with caller-supplied `Config`. `Config::m_MaxConnectionsPerName` caps the total live connections (idle + active) per named pool entry. `Config::m_MaxIdleTime` determines how long an idle handle survives before eviction on next `Acquire` or `EvictStale`. `Config::m_AcquireTimeout` is the maximum time `Acquire` will block waiting for a slot when the pool is full.

- `~CloudConnectionPool()` — Calls `DrainAll()`, destroying all idle handles via registered `DestroyFn` callbacks. **Does not wait for handles currently checked out** (i.e., active connections not yet `Release`d are leaked at destruction).

- `RegisterType(type, destroyFn, healthCheckFn)` — Associates a connection type string (e.g., `"postgres"`) with a `DestroyFn` (`void(void*)`) and a `HealthCheckFn` (`bool(void*)`). Must be called before any `Acquire` for that type if health checking or proper teardown is required. Acquiring without a registered type skips health checks and does not call `Destroy` on evicted handles. Takes the mutex; safe to call from any thread before pool use.

- `Acquire(connectionName, type, createFn, errorMessage) → PooledHandle` — Returns a checked-out handle for `connectionName`. Algorithm: (1) walks the idle deque for `connectionName`, discarding any handle whose idle time exceeds `m_MaxIdleTime` or that fails its health check — both cases invoke `DestroyFn`; (2) returns the first surviving handle with `m_Valid = true` and increments `m_ActiveCount`; (3) if no idle handle exists and the pool is at capacity, blocks on the condition variable up to `m_AcquireTimeout`; (4) on timeout, populates `errorMessage` and returns a zero-initialised (falsy) `PooledHandle`; (5) if a slot is available and no idle handle was released during the wait, drops the lock and calls `createFn(errorMessage)` to allocate a new connection, then re-acquires the lock, increments `m_ActiveCount`, and returns the new handle. `createFn` is invoked **outside the mutex**. Callers must check `operator bool()` on the returned handle before use.

- `Release(connectionName, type, handle)` — Returns a checked-out handle to the idle deque. Decrements `m_ActiveCount` (floor 0). If `handle.m_Valid && handle.m_Connection`, updates `m_LastUsed` to now and pushes it onto the back of the idle deque. Notifies one waiting `Acquire` caller via the condition variable. Passing an invalid or null handle is safe — it just adjusts the active count and signals a waiter.

- `EvictStale()` — Scans all idle deques under the mutex and destroys handles whose `m_LastUsed` age exceeds `m_MaxIdleTime`. **Destroy callback selection is a known approximation**: it iterates `m_TypeCallbacks` and uses the first registered type's `DestroyFn` regardless of what type the handle actually belongs to. This is correct only when a connection name maps to a single registered type. Intended to be called periodically from an external scheduler (no internal timer exists).

- `Drain(connectionName)` — Destroys and removes all idle handles for the specified connection name. Uses the same first-registered-type destroy approximation as `EvictStale`. Does not affect active (checked-out) connections. Logs a `LOG_APP_INFO` message on completion.

- `DrainAll()` — Destroys all idle handles across all pool entries, using the same destroy approximation. Called by the destructor. Logs a `LOG_APP_INFO` message.

---

**Collaborators:**

- `engine.h` — Provides the `LOG_APP_INFO` and `LOG_APP_WARN` macros used for operational logging throughout the implementation. No other direct API from `engine.h` is used in this file.
- **libpq (PostgreSQL C client)** — The primary intended consumer. Callers supply a `CreateFn` that calls `PQconnectdb` (or equivalent), a `HealthCheckFn` that calls `PQstatus`, and a `DestroyFn` that calls `PQfinish`. The pool itself has no libpq dependency.
- **libcurl** — Explicitly not a consumer; the file comment notes that libcurl handles its own connection reuse. This pool is not used for HTTP connectors.
- Future IMAP keep-alive integration is mentioned in comments as a planned consumer.

---

**Threading / lifetime:**

- All public methods acquire `m_Mutex` (a `std::mutex`) before accessing pool state. `m_Cv` (a `std::condition_variable`) is used only inside `Acquire` and signalled by `Release`.
- `createFn` is called with the mutex **released** to avoid holding the lock during a potentially blocking network connect. The lock is re-acquired immediately after `createFn` returns, so `m_ActiveCount` is updated atomically with respect to other threads after connection creation.
- `PooledHandle` is a value type with no internal synchronisation. Callers own the handle from the moment `Acquire` returns until `Release` is called. **Do not share a single handle across threads.**
- `CloudConnectionPool` itself has no specific ownership model enforced; it is expected to be held as a member of a longer-lived service object (e.g., a cloud provider client) and outlive all active handles.
- **Destruction is unsafe if active handles are outstanding.** The destructor only drains the idle queue. Any `void*` still checked out at destruction time will not have `DestroyFn` called, resulting in a resource leak.

---

**Notes:**

- **Destroy callback approximation in `EvictStale`, `Drain`, and `DrainAll`:** All three methods select the first `DestroyFn` found by iterating `m_TypeCallbacks` (unordered, so iteration order is undefined) rather than the destroy function registered for the specific type of each handle. If more than one type is registered, the wrong destructor may be called on some handles. This is a correctness bug when the pool is shared across heterogeneous connection types under the same `CloudConnectionPool` instance.
- **`m_MaxConnectionsPerName` counts idle + active.** In `Acquire`, the capacity check computes `total = active + idle.size()`. However, after a successful `wait_for`, the post-wait retry path does not re-run the stale/health-check loop; it naively pops the front of the idle deque and returns it without staleness or health validation, potentially returning a dead connection.
- **No internal eviction timer.** `EvictStale()` is passive; the caller is responsible for scheduling it (e.g., via a periodic background task in the engine).
- **`RegisterType` overwrites silently.** Calling `RegisterType` a second time for the same type string replaces the callbacks with no warning, which will affect subsequent health checks and destructions for all existing idle handles of that type.
- **`operator bool()` on `PooledHandle`** requires both `m_Valid == true` and `m_Connection != nullptr`. A default-constructed or timeout-result handle has `m_Valid = false`, so the bool check is the correct way to detect failure from `Acquire`.
- The idle deque is ordered oldest-first (push_back on release, pop_front on acquire), giving an approximate LRU eviction ordering when combined with `EvictStale`.
